#### Abstracts
##### **HiVe-Mem: Interleaving Horizontal Memory Expansion and Vertical Evidence Composition for Multimodal Agents**
2611.12872v1 by Haozhen Zhang, Bohan Liu, Jianzhu Bao, Qingyuan Liu, Haodong Yue, Xiao Li, Zi Pong Lim, Wenya Wang

As multimodal agents accumulate increasingly long interaction histories, effectively retrieving and utilizing past information becomes critical for long-term interaction. Existing memory systems typically rely on semantic retrieval over compressed memories and expose predefined representations, which may miss relevant memories and overlook heterogeneous evidence needs across queries. We introduce \textbf{\hivemem}, a multimodal memory framework that interleaves \textbf{\textsc{H}}or\textbf{\textsc{i}}zontal Memory Expansion with \textbf{\textsc{Ve}}rtical Evidence Composition. For horizontal memory expansion, \hivemem organizes memory episodes into a relational graph using textual and visual anchors, expanding semantically retrieved seeds with related neighbors to recover complementary information. For vertical evidence composition, each retrieved memory retains multiple evidence forms, including compact summaries, captions, grounded visual views, raw images, and interaction history. A lightweight trainable selector independently composes an evidence subset for each retrieved memory, enabling query-adaptive utilization of heterogeneous multimodal information. This two-dimensional design jointly broadens memory coverage and adapts the evidence contributed by individual memories. Experiments across three multimodal memory benchmarks show that \hivemem consistently improves task performance across base models while reducing token consumption by about 90\% compared with the most efficient competing multimodal baselines.

摘要：

##### **Is Persona Generalization Predictable?**
2611.12864v1 by Olina Mukherjee, Arnav Goel, Aditi Raghunathan, Jacob Mitchell Springer

Persona generalization occurs when fine-tuning a language model in one domain induces behavioral changes in others -- for example, the broadly unsafe behavior observed in emergent misalignment. We discover a surprising "on-the-line" phenomenon in persona generalization: in-domain and out-of-domain persona rates across various fine-tuning hyperparameters follow an approximately probit-linear relationship, and the specific probit-linear relationship is surprisingly shared even across model families and model sizes, including Qwen and Llama. Training choices that leave the set of trainable parameters unchanged, such as learning rate, weight decay, and training duration, largely move models along the same line, reducing transfer mainly by weakening in-domain learning. Changing which layers are fine-tuned, however, can change the slope. For unsafe behavior, fine-tuning only later layers yields shallower slopes, retaining strong in-domain learning while reducing out-of-domain misalignment. This "on-the-line" phenomenon unifies unexpected, isolated observations of persona generalization within a quantitative framework for predicting and controlling behavioral transfer.

摘要：

##### **WorkflowArena: Benchmarking Executable Workflow Orchestration in LLM-Based Multi-Agent Systems**
2611.12856v1 by Zheng Jiang, Fang Niu, Bo Peng, Houde Qian, Wenyi Li, Deyao Hong, Mingju Gao, Yizhe Chi, Youjie Zheng, Kaisen Yang, Calvin Xiao, Lifeng Sun, Huan-ang Gao

Large language model agents are increasingly deployed as multi-agent systems rather than as a standalone model operating within a single context. Recent agent harnesses allow the model to instantiate worker agents and to author the executable workflow that coordinates them, shifting the design of system structure from developers to the model at run time. The quality of this organization therefore constitutes a distinct capability, yet existing benchmarks either measure end-to-end task completion or evaluate coordination under structures specified in advance. We formalize this capability as workflow orchestration and introduce WorkflowArena, a benchmark of 145 long-horizon tasks spanning 8 professional domains, from software engineering and formal mathematics to legal analysis and 3D production. Each task specifies an initial workspace and an integrated deliverable composed of interdependent components, which is assessed by a hidden verifier. Agents determine autonomously whether and how to delegate, and all delegation is recorded, enabling joint evaluation of deliverable quality and the organization of the underlying work. Experiments with nine models show that no single configuration performs best across all domains and that systems exhibit distinct delegation strategies: at high effort, Claude models in Claude Code invoke workflows less frequently but with larger worker groups, whereas GPT models in Codex delegate more frequently in smaller units. When the executing model is held fixed on a challenging subset of tasks, workflows authored by Claude Opus 5.5 substantially improve weaker executors, indicating that workflow organization is itself a measurable determinant of task performance.

##### **Programming Prescience: Issues in Human Construction of Forecasting Bots**
2611.12834v1 by Priya Dhawka, Benjamin Wilson, Andrew Head

AI models' growing capacity for complex reasoning has made them attractive tools for forecasting, or the systematic prediction of the future based on past and present data. However, should AI forecasting systems be adopted, human builders will shoulder the work of building and evaluating them. In this paper, we explore what this work looks like with a qualitative interview study with 15 builders of forecasting bots, sampled from among successful participants in a recent AI forecasting competition. Our analysis surfaces the challenges around (1) tuning a bot to acquire factual research, (2) implementing coherent reasoning, and (3) understanding how well one's bot actually does. During their work, builders frequently introduced complexity that did not pay off, and tested in ways that were opportunistic and piecemeal. Our detailed account of their challenges helps clarify what problems should be targeted in upcoming innovations in process, tools, and human-AI teaming for forecasting.

##### **How Well Does AI Peer Review Work? A Benchmark Built from 100 Planted Errors**
2611.12820v1 by Paul Litvak

One hundred known errors were planted into 10 open-access psychology papers, and the modified papers were then run through frontier models and two commercial AI review tools. The best single system caught 71 of the 100 errors, while the worst caught 30. Pooling every system's output caught 93. Models are only partly correlated in the errors they find, which makes ensembling a big lever for finding issues in papers. Seven errors could not be caught by any system, and all seven were omissions: information deleted from a paper rather than mistakes inserted into it. Refine.ink contributes more unique catches than any other single system, though it is expensive on a per-error basis. False positives were not measured, and how this error distribution compares to the distribution of errors in real papers remains unknown. The papers, errors, model outputs, and full experiment log are public, so that others can build on this toward a comprehensive evaluation benchmark across disciplines.

Mining this pool raises two challenges: i) which checkpoint to distill from, as no checkpoint dominates the others and complementarity to the student is problem-specific; ii) where to trust it, as even a well-chosen teacher is only locally superior, so indiscriminate distillation overwrites behavior the student has already mastered. Fine-tuning raises chrF++ from 10-14 to 29.0-30.6 and lowers BPB from 2.23-2.41 to 0.78-0.84; most zero-shot outputs (54-92%) are English-like. Likelihood and overlap disagree on how far apart the models are. Llama-3.2-3B has the lowest BPB in every seed, 0.042 below Qwen2.5-3B (95% CI 0.039-0.046), yet the two cannot be separated on chrF++ (30.51 and 30.61) or BLEU, even with paired item-level tests. Gemma-2-2B, whose tokenizer splits Romanized Nepali words least (2.72 tokens per word), scores worst on every measure. Which model gains most over its zero-shot baseline depends on the zero-shot prompt. In a supplementary comparison with an identical tokenizer, Llama-3.1-8B lowers BPB by 0.053 (0.050-0.056) relative to Llama-3.2-3B, while chrF++ and BLEU rise by only 0.68 and 0.58 points. Lower fertility and higher reference likelihood therefore did not identify the model whose outputs were closest to the references.

##### **Type-logical grammars and formal languages**
2611.12618v1 by Richard Moot

We show that type-logical grammars with the standard rules for product, associativity, extraction and infixation have an NP-complete fixed recognition problem, adapting a construction for first-order linear logic from Pshenitsyn (2025), who already showed that grammars in first-order linear logic are closed under intersection and therefore not semilinear and not mildly context-sensitive. We strengthen these results and shown that grammars in first-order linear logic can simulate the quasi-linear grammars of Book \& Greibach (1970), the grammars recognised in non-deterministic linear time (NSPACE(n) or. NLIN). I argue that NLIN is an interesting class of formal languages as well, and a useful upper bound on the time complexity of formalisms used for natural language, just like LOGCFL is a useful upper bound on the space complexity. Taken together this means that first-order linear logic can be seen as the logic of non-deterministic linear-time string languages, graph languages and automata.

##### **Double Blind Evals: Resolving the Dual Confidentiality Dilemma in AI Safety Auditing**
2611.12604v1 by Andrew Trask, Sol Messing, Vai Pahwa, Pegah Maham, Rene Kolga, Austin Frantz, Andrew Tash, Kate Thomas, Sean McGregor, George Balston, Patricia Paskov, Miles Brundage, Akriti Vij, Bennett Hillenbrand, Alexandros Karargyris, Tin Acosta, Joel Fenster, Madeleine Elish, Rasmi Elasmar, Myriam Khan, Koen van der Veen, Rasswanth S, Sameer Wagh, Stephen Gabriel, Pedro Werneck, Lacey Strahm, Kevin McDonough, Ronnie Falcon, Kristian Lum, William Isaac

AI safety evaluations often involve sensitive prompts and proprietary models. Evaluators want to keep their evaluation prompts secret from model owners to prevent purposeful or incidental data leakage, ensuring longer usefulness of their benchmark. Model owners in turn wish to keep their proprietary model weights and inference code within their own secure infrastructure to protect their IP. We present a way forward for "double-blind evals", which provide cryptographic guarantees that an evaluator's prompts are not shared with the model owner, while simultaneously maintaining the confidentiality of the model weights and inference code. To accomplish this, we use secure enclaves that encrypt data in RAM at the hardware level. In a world-first pilot, we test this approach by evaluating a proprietary Google DeepMind model (Gemini 2.5 Flash Lite) against a selection of the private AILuminate benchmark corpus, using an NVIDIA H100 secure enclave on Google Cloud Platform, and using OpenMined's PySyft to maintain data and model privacy. We believe this effort marks the first use of this technology to provide mutual secrecy for one organization's frontier-class proprietary model weights and another organization's closed benchmark.

##### **Fairness in Explainable AI Narratives: Decomposing Social Disparities in LLM-Generated Explanations**
2611.12592v1 by Wannes Van den Bulck, Sofie Goethals, David Martens

Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to translate technical explainable AI (XAI) artefacts such as SHAP into explanatory narratives (XAINs) for lay users. In high-risk domains, these explanations are often the main resource through which individuals can understand and contest adverse decisions. Yet LLMs are known to reproduce social bias, even from proxy variables alone, and fairness in XAINs remains unstudied. Because XAINs are bound to a ground-truth artefact, fairness concerns not only what a narrative says and how, but also whether it is equally faithful across social groups. We introduce fairness in XAINs as a new field of research and propose a framework that generates narratives under three conditions (protected attribute disclosed, withheld, or counterfactually swapped), allowing five comparisons that pinpoint the cause of disparities. We apply it to adversely classified instances from four datasets in credit scoring, education and employment, generating narratives with five LLMs, and evaluate faithfulness, feature coverage and sentiment. We find significant disparities across all datasets, metric families and models, arising in two ways. Faithfulness disparities result from feature values: LLMs make plausible but false statements, triggered by values that are unequally distributed across social groups. Feature coverage and sentiment disparities stem from the protected attribute itself: once it is disclosed, LLMs give less explanation to, or frame more negatively the narratives of, groups that have historically been discriminated against or are stereotypically associated with worse outcomes. A more faithful model removes the faithfulness disparities, and withholding protected attributes mostly removes the others, but neither suffices on its own. Fairness in XAINs should therefore be audited separately from the predictive model and its XAI artefact.

##### **Evaluating Cross-Signal Coherence in Mammography Vision-Language Models**
2611.12574v1 by Rebeca Nam, Chase M. Fensore, Christian A. Fauria-Robinson, Judy Gichoya, Joyce C. Ho

As vision-language models (VLMs) move toward interpreting medical images directly, their outputs increasingly include not just a label but a stated confidence and a written justification. Yet the field still predominantly evaluates VLMs using just accuracy. We evaluate the coherence between answer, confidence, and explanation across four VLMs on breast density and BI-RADS categorization, using two mammography datasets. We perform controlled perturbations of the question, multiple choice option orders, and the images and assess changes in the three outputs. Our results suggest confidence does not decrease even as the mammography image is changed. Shuffling the options also changes most VLMs' answers. Notably, there are cases where a model's own stated reasoning contradicts its final answer, a distinction accuracy alone cannot capture but one that governs whether an AI-generated answer can be trusted.

##### **WanderBench: Can World Models Explore Dynamic Worlds from Video?**
2611.12557v1 by Yuhang Chen, Jiheng Li, Jiale Wang, Zai Wang, Guangyue Liu

Video world models conditioned on an input video make it possible to explore real, recorded dynamic scenes, enabling re-shooting of footage, free-viewpoint replay, and real-to-sim environments for embodied agents. Such uses require the generated rollout to stay faithful to the recorded world, yet existing benchmarks start from a single image or a text prompt, and video-conditioned generation is evaluated mainly on perceptual quality and camera following. These evaluations do not directly assess whether exploration preserves subject identity, event progression, and scene consistency upon revisiting a location. We introduce WanderBench, a benchmark for exploring dynamic worlds from video. It comprises 745 single-shot source videos of real and AI-generated content in three difficulty tiers (3-25 s), paired with camera routes given as keyboard actions, and evaluates six dimensions with 24 human-validated sub-metrics. Benchmarking eight models, we find that as clips grow longer, models keep the static scene and visual quality but lose camera control and the source dynamics, and that none keeps revisited places consistent. We further propose WanderLand, a training-free framework that conditions each frame of a frozen model on the same-instant source and, on revisits, on its own earlier static content. WanderLand improves five models on every dimension, by up to 8.3 points in source-dynamics fidelity.

##### **WaveGen: Truly End-to-End Waveform Generation via Internal Spectral Trajectory Learning**
2611.12527v1 by Sang-Hoon Lee, Ha-Yeong Choi

This work proposes WaveGen, a fully end-to-end diffusion-based text-to-waveform framework based on Spectral Forcing, which jointly learns an internal spectral trajectory and the target waveform trajectory within a single model, enabling direct end-to-end text-to-waveform generation. Unlike recent TTS pipelines, WaveGen does not rely on separately trained or externally decoded intermediate representations, such as neural audio codecs, Mel-spectrogram vocoders, or VAE-based latent models. In addition, WaveGen performs internal text-speech alignment within a single model, eliminating the need for external alignment modules such as duration predictors. To preserve a truly end-to-end formulation, our framework further avoids dependence on self-supervised representations, such as BERT-based text embeddings and Wav2Vec 2.0-based semantic representations, as well as semantic distillation methods for training optimization. To achieve this, we carefully design Spectral Forcing, a model architecture with disentangled diffusion heads, and a training objective based on a multi-scale spectral $v$-loss. We further validate the scalability of end-to-end TTS models by scaling the model size from 0.1B to 0.6B parameters. Using a single architecture, open-source data, and one-stage training, without external modules such as duration predictor or REPA, WaveGen demonstrates fully end-to-end waveform generation achieving state-of-the-art performance on the Emilia training dataset. In particular, WaveGen achieves a WER of 1.47 and a SIM of 0.75 on the Seed-en benchmark. Source code and checkpoints are available at https://github.com/sh-lee-prml/WaveGen

##### **Can the Confidence and Decisions of System 1 Models Be Trusted? An Explainability and Perturbation Audit of Jev-Style Decision Models**
2611.12521v1 by Julien Delaunay

"System 1" decision models (pioneered by TypeSafe AI's Jev) replace autoregressive text generation with a single forward pass returning typed probability distributions over declared schemas (Choice, Score, Noul). While deployed for low-latency routing and automated triage, their statistical calibration and post-hoc explainability remain uncharacterized. We introduce an open, deterministic audit benchmark evaluating System 1 decision models across calibration under label-preserving perturbations, cross-question interference, and post-hoc attribution faithfulness. ##### **MARS: Multiscale Appearance-Residual Streams for Video Forgery Detection**
2611.12508v1 by Ahmed Y. Radwan, Shaina Raza

Generalizing deepfake detection across facial manipulation and fully generated video remains challenging due to forgery traces varying with the manipulation method and scene content. Explicit spatial and temporal feature differences offer a way to capture this variation, but also respond to natural texture and motion, motivating their joint interpretation with appearance features. We investigate whether combining multiscale feature differences with adapted appearance representations improves detection across both forgery categories. We introduce Multiscale Appearance-Residual Streams (MARS), a two-path video detector. The residual path applies fixed spatial and temporal difference-of-Gaussians operators to frozen intermediate features and models the resulting differences alongside appearance context across frames. The semantic path adapts the encoder's normalization parameters for frame classification, and a clip-dependent gate combines their predictions. Under a shared protocol across seven datasets, MARS achieves the highest average AUROC of 91.43\%, outperforming seven compared detectors. When trained on facial manipulations alone, MARS achieves 93.99\% AUROC on GenVideo and 75.50\% on DF26, demonstrating transfer to generated videos without generated-video training data. MARS also provides signed patch and frame contributions, whose faithfulness we evaluate through controlled spatial and temporal removal against random controls. MARS is publicly available for research: Project page: https://vectorinstitute.github.io/mars/ Github: https://github.com/VectorInstitute/mars

##### **Pricing Symbolic Rules by What GNNs Leave Unlearned**
2611.12446v1 by Satabarto Sarkar, Prithwineel Paul

Symbolic knowledge (motif counts, ring counts, label constraints) is routinely added to graph neural networks (GNNs) and justified by their limited expressivity. We replace this yardstick with an exact one. The largest log-loss reduction a rule $S$ can give a model family is $I(Y;S\midσ)$ given what the family computes; every realised gain splits exactly into this term plus repaid neural debt; and for a trained network with representation $T$ the value is $I(Y;S\mid T)$, the label information training failed to extract. Rule design thus becomes rule discovery: we score candidates on the network's out-of-fold residual against calibrated permutation nulls with family-wise error control. On ZINC, discovery recovers the hidden ring term $\max(0,\ell_{\max}-6)$ of the target in three independent runs ($p_{\mathrm{adj}}=0.001$) and beats expert cycle counts; frozen and applied to five unseen datasets it is never significantly worse than the plain network or the expert rule, and on BBBP it significantly beats both.

Probabilistic circuits (PCs) have emerged as generative models guaranteeing tractable probabilistic inference. In this work, we revisit this practice to highlight a surprising learnability issue: models that assume independence counterintuitively can yield better likelihoods than models that do not. To break this apparent bottleneck and to scale PCs further, we introduce a recursive parameter sharing construction (RePA) that fosters efficiency without assuming independence. ##### **To SIMD or Not to SIMD: Comparing RVV vs. CGRA SoCs for Non-Linear Functions in AI**
2611.12435v1 by Sameer Deshmukh, Naoya Mizuki, Takumi Honda

Non-linear functions are an important class of operations in modern large language models. Reconfigurable units such as FPGAs and CGRAs have been proposed in the literature, but have not been systematically compared with traditional units backed by MMUs. In this paper, we build two SoCs: one using an RVV vector processor and the other using a latency-insensitive (\emph{elastic}) CGRA, both coupled with an MMU and driven by a single-core CPU. We then propose mappings for common feed-forward networks on our SoCs and find that the CGRA reaches a maximum speedup in clock cycles of 7.12x over RVV on the activation stage of simple point-wise non-linear functions like SiLU, whereas the RVV reaches a maximum speedup of \textbf{9.8x} over the CGRA on complex non-linear functions such as $\mathrm{softmax}$. ##### **dNaViT: Discrete Codes are General-Purpose Visual Representations**

Discrete autoregressive modeling, notably through next-token prediction, has become a cornerstone of modern language models. Existing research tells the agents that partners exist or asks them to coordinate. Whether coordination arises through shared state alone, with nothing disclosing that anyone else is present, is the key question for deployments that share caches and memory between agents, yet it has rarely been examined, and never with independent replication; this study does both. Four agents each received a short arithmetic task and a key-value store presented to each as its own scratch space; two held complementary halves of one computation, and no prompt mentioned other agents, sharing or collaboration. For reliability, the environment was rebuilt independently from a written protocol, and both implementations ran under hashed locks on design, data and analysis. The results bear novelty value in three respects. Joint success occurred only through the shared store. Whether a model coordinated depended not on its vendor tier but on whether it wrote its intermediate result and looked at the store again, and one ordinary sentence, "check your workspace again before submitting", switched that second look on and off. The findings held under replication. For practice, state shared between agents is a communication channel and should be separated, namespaced and logged. For research, designs should be locked before the data, and the parse rule, endpoint and parameters reported. ##### **AutoBuddy: Evaluating Proactive, Always-On Agents on Task Discovery and Execution in a Simulated Workplace**

We introduce AutoBuddy-Bench, a simulator that addresses these challenges. It models an organisation's employees, projects, business systems and event history as a temporal graph, renders it into realistic workspace artefacts, derives ground-truth tasks from the events that create obligations, and computes each task's outcome from the world state; execution is judged by the agent's changes to the workspace. External validation, calibration analysis and reporting guidelines adherence remain prerequisites for clinical adoption.

##### **Reconstructing hourly traffic on every street of a capital from a sparse counting network: a graph neural network applied to Paris**

Micro-scale air quality models require hourly traffic data on every road segment, whereas permanent counters cover only a small part of the network. The model adapts the Graph Neural Network for Urban Interpolation (GNNUI) to a real, sparse counting network. Flows are decoded on the edges of the junction graph (one edge per segment, both directions summed), a traffic climatology serves as an offset in a zero-inflated negative-binomial likelihood, and training proceeds by masking counters. Eight-fold spatial cross-validation, with counters sharing a published series merged before splitting, distinguishes interpolation at a withheld counter in an instrumented district (R${}^2$ = 0.89, error of a quarter of the mean flow) from extrapolation to a district without counters (R${}^2$ = 0.74, a third of the mean flow). These scores hold on main axes where counters are located, while relative errors are about 50% on the few counted local streets. ##### **Detecting Shadow AI: Unsanctioned LLM Agents on Enterprise Endpoints: A Taxonomy, an Open Detector and a Controlled Evaluation**
2611.12377v1 by Jeremy Canale, Fabien Soulis

Shadow IT, the use of technology that the IT organisation has not approved, is a long-standing governance problem. We argue that autonomous AI agents can make it grow much faster: agents make building tools nearly free, act with their user's privileges, and leave behind scripts, automations and MCP servers that are themselves unsanctioned. A simple compounding model shows that, under its assumptions, shadow IT grows exponentially once agentic artifacts reproduce faster than the organisation retires them; retiring them starts with detecting them. We then ask whether unsanctioned agents can be detected on Windows endpoints. We propose a taxonomy of nine classes of agents and six layers of endpoint footprint, and argue that rules based only on the structure of processes and connections cannot separate an agent from its behavioural twin, the same program talking to a non-AI service. We present Agent Detector, an open-source Windows detector that anchors behaviour to the identity of the remote service and correlates it with configuration, installation and execution history, using a catalog of 39 tools and 184 LLM endpoint entries; it informs users through a notice they must acknowledge instead of blocking. On a controlled bench of 12 synthetic agents, 3 AI components and 9 benign workloads, repeated 5 times on one workstation, two behavioural rules flagged every behavioural twin, while Agent Detector flagged none and detected every agent in the majority of runs. Its limits are temporal: a connection to a provider behind a shared content-delivery network is attributable only while the DNS cache holds the provider's name, one to five minutes, and an intermittent agent was seen by about one scan in three. ##### **DForkTree: Accelerating Speculative Decoding with Fork-Aware Block Diffusion Draft Trees**
2611.12356v1 by Zhicheng Zheng, Cheng Zhang, Chenkai Tang

Speculative decoding speeds up language models by letting a lightweight drafter propose several future tokens that the target model verifies in parallel. Block diffusion drafters such as DFlash draft a whole block in one forward pass, and DDTree builds a draft tree from their per-position distributions. Each of these distributions assumes that the drafter's own most likely continuation is correct. A tree, however, gains over a chain only on branches that leave this continuation, where the drafter's top-1 accuracy drops from 89% to 18% and its candidate lists miss the right token far more often. DForkTree adds n-gram statistics from the target model's own outputs to the drafter's scores as pointwise mutual information, weights them more after the fork, and builds the optimal tree under these scores exactly on the GPU. On Qwen3.5-4B and 9B, with all tree methods sharing one drafter, tree pipeline and verifier, DForkTree is the fastest tree method on every benchmark. ##### **Evidence or Marginal? What Moment-Vector Propagation Computes in Polynomial Density Neurons**
2611.12339v1 by Przemysław Spyra

When a neuron's input is lost, or known through a belief $q$, what should it output? For a density model both are exact answers to different questions: $p_\mathrm{CD}$ is Bayesian updating with the belief as virtual evidence; $p_\mathrm{EU}$ installs the belief as a new marginal (Jeffrey's rule). With a missing input $p_\mathrm{CD}$ returns the model's conditional; $p_\mathrm{EU}$ generally does not. We challenge this assumption and argue that the main limitation lies not in the raw representation itself, but in the absence of a learning objective that separates structured signal from noise. Once removed, such information cannot be recovered by supervised learning. ##### **Do Machine Learning Models Predict?**
2611.12332v1 by Michele Pizzochero, Emanuele Ratti

Machine learning (ML) models are said to predict. Philosophers draw a line between accommodation and prediction, depending on whether or not the evidence a theory succeeds on was used in constructing it. Does this distinction survive when a theory is replaced by an ML model? We argue so, yet the dividing line becomes fuzzy. Whether an output of an ML model counts as an accommodation or as a prediction hinges upon the relationship between the patterns the input instantiate and those on which the model was selected through its training. Once this relation is taken into account, most successful outputs of ML models, we suggest, bear a strong accommodation character. We further trace the consequences of our account for confirmation, novelty, and scientific realism.

Finally, hardware-specific optimizations in the MNN inference engine enable fast, memory-efficient inference and long-context processing. Extensive evaluations demonstrate that MNN-Pocket-0.3B achieves frontier-level performance among models of comparable size, with particularly strong results in Chinese and English knowledge and reasoning, mathematics, coding, and instruction following. It consistently outperforms similarly sized open-source models and even surpasses several 0.5B-parameter models on instruction-following benchmarks. We release MNN-Pocket-0.3B with open weights and ready-to-use MNN deployment packages. While the technical architecture of climate services increasingly relies on advanced machine learning and large-scale climate models, their legitimacy as public services may be weakened in the absence of arrangements that prioritise public value and long-lasting societal benefit over profit maximisation. The tension between the public mandate of climate services and the private nature of AI infrastructure challenges traditional notions of openness. Using a consensus-based elicitation method coupled with the PESTLE framework, we analyse the political, economic, social, technological, legal, and environmental risks and opportunities shaping the co-production value chain of AI-enabled climate services. We draw policy-relevant conclusions for data, model and infrastructure supporting the next generation of upstream and downstream climate services as critical applications for society. Our work presents potential and plausible challenges and benefits for a pan-European, decentralised public climate service built on sovereign AI infrastructure and open governance principles.

摘要：

##### **From Fluent Text to Ontology-Compatible Definitions: A Quality-Control Agentic Workflow**
2611.12095v1 by Kun Feng, Chenchen Chu, Yan Liu, Haojie Sun, Jijun Tang, Chen Tian, Shuolin Xiao, Jianping He, Zhao Li

Biomedical ontologies are structured knowledge systems that must evolve continuously to represent newly emerging knowledge, but their manual maintenance remains time-consuming and laborintensive. Textual definitions are especially costly because they must be readable to humans and compatible with controlled entities, relations, and logical constraints. Large language models (LLMs) offer a promising way to assist ontology engineering tasks, including definition generation. However, textual plausibility does not guarantee knowledge-system compatibility. A generated definition may be fluent and close to a reference definition, yet still fail to ground its implied entities or violate ontology-level constraints. Directly inserting such definitions into ontology libraries may therefore introduce entity-level hallucinations and logical inconsistencies. To address this problem, we propose two structured-knowledge incompatibility metrics, Entity Error Rate (EER) and Logical Inconsistency Rate (LIR), to quantify entity grounding and logical consistency for LLM-generated ontology definitions. We further develop an agentic quality-control (QC) workflow that treats definition generation as an iterative process rather than a direct decoding step: it generates definitions, extracts axioms, checks entity existence and logical consistency, and uses the resulting diagnostics to drive repair loops. Comprehensive experiments on Disease Ontology, Uberon Ontology, and Uberon-ConstraintStress demonstrate that EER and LIR complement conventional reference-based metrics by evaluating whether generated content can be grounded and checked inside the target ontology. The workflow effectively decreases EER while maintaining strong conventional text-generation performance. LIR further exposes sparse ontology-level logical failures that are invisible to text similarity metrics, although their repair remains limited.

摘要：

##### **CRAFT-AI: Capability and Requirement Alignment for Flexible Manufacturing through Agentic AI**
2611.12092v1 by Kiavash Fathi, Hans Wernher van de Venn

One of the fundamental needs for reaching resilient production infrastructure is flexible manufacturing, which requires translating changing production requirements into implementable system configurations. This paper introduces \textbf{CRAFT-AI}, an agentic AI approach combining natural language (NL) requirement interpretation, Asset Administration Shell (AAS)-based capability descriptions, and reusable engineering knowledge from existing manufacturing configurations. A tool-using AI agent matches requirements with available capabilities and reference implementations to generate manufacturing scenes and corresponding AAS instances. The approach is demonstrated using a Factory I/O prototype with twelve reference scenes and five illustrative manufacturing tasks of varying complexity, providing an initial feasibility assessment of the proposed AAS-enabled, system-level generative engineering approach. For the four valid user task descriptions, proper requirements, correct references to the relevant engineering knowledge, correct components and lastly a valid Factory I/O scene are generated. For the task with missing engineering knowledge and a wrong sensor selection, the user is informed about which requirements of the scene generation lack domain knowledge and also which sensor is not compatible with the selected raw material by the user with link to the related documentation. After fixing the task description, the correct scene is seamlessly generated. \textbf{CRAFT-AI} highlights the importance of safeguards for risk perception and communication in foundation model-driven solutions.

摘要：

##### **SadaqahStories: A Verified English-Arabic Corpus of Charity Stories for Children, with an Audit of Errors in AI-Drafted Religious Narratives**
2611.12078v1 by Wajdi Zaghouani, Spencer Striker

We present SadaqahStories, a parallel English-Arabic corpus of 41 illustrated four-page stories about charitable giving in Islam (Sadaqah, Zakat and Waqf), written for readers aged 7 to 18. The stories were first drafted with large language model (LLM) assistance, then audited against hadith collections, Qur'an references and historical sources, and finally rewritten for children and signed off by an Islamic scholar. Every story carries an evidence label and links to its sources. We release all three versions of each story (AI-assisted draft, audited text, final child-oriented text) together with the correction log, so the corpus can be used to study how AI drafts of religious and historical content go wrong. The audit found substantive errors in 39 of the 41 drafts. The most frequent were unsupported specific details (dates, figures, prices, names), invented quotations, institutions or anecdotes, and conflation of separate events or people; six drafts contained errors in the wording, grading or ruling of a religious text. Rewriting cut the estimated Flesch-Kincaid grade of the English text from 14.0 to 6.1. We also release an 81-entry charity lexicon covering Modern Standard Arabic and Qatari Arabic, and describe the free, account-free website that serves the corpus (https://sadaqahjourney.com).

摘要：

##### **How a Model's Architecture and Performance Influences Explainability: A Study of Grad-CAM and CRAFT in CNN-Based Image Classification**
2611.12075v1 by Kilian Göller, Haadia Amjad, Steffen Seitz, Carsten Knoll, Ronald Tetzlaff

Deep learning models, particularly convolutional neural networks (CNNs), are widely adopted in computer vision, yet their opaque decision-making raises concerns in high-risk applications like healthcare and autonomous systems, where trust and accountability are critical. Therefore, robust explainable AI (XAI) methods are desirable to provide transparent insights into model decisions. To ensure these methods are reliable and effective across diverse scenarios, it is important to understand how model performance and architectural complexity influence explanation quality. Our study takes an initial step toward systematically investigating the impact of these two factors on key XAI evaluation metrics. We constructed a pipeline using 10 subsets of ImageNet-1K, each containing 80 unique classes, on each of which we trained three CNN architectures of increasing structural complexity: VGG16, ResNet50, and ConvNeXt-Tiny. For each individual model, we analyzed 10 training checkpoints representing increasing levels of model accuracy using two XAI methods: Grad-CAM (feature-attribution-based) and CRAFT (concept-based). We assessed these methods across four evaluation categories: Faithfulness, Correctness, Sensitivity, and Simplicity. Our findings reveal that as training progresses, the complex architectures (ResNet50 and ConvNeXt-Tiny) exhibit improved Faithfulness and lower Sensitivity, indicating more robust explanations. In contrast, VGG16's simpler architecture yields simpler but less faithful and correct explanations.

摘要：

##### **MIRIX-2.0: Multi-Agent Memory System**
2611.12068v1 by Wei-Chieh Huang, Weizhi Zhang, Jiayan Nan, Peng Xia, Yu Wang

Long-term memory lets LLM agents personalize, stay consistent, and reuse what worked before. Parametric memories need training, consolidation can lose details for good, and keeping every record leaves a growing store to search. We present MIRIX-2.0, a multi-agent memory system designed to be practical, accurate, and efficient at once. It is the most accurate of the compared systems on four of five QA benchmarks. It reaches 91.4% on LoCoMo and 72.6% on LongMemEval-S, where it leads the strongest baseline by 14.3 points, and with AutoDream disabled it gains 31.0 and 22.0 points over HyperMem on single-hop and multi-hop document QA. As procedural memory, it yields the highest mean success among the compared memory methods on ALFWorld and WebShop and reaches 49.2% on MetaClaw-Bench, against 44.0% for MetaClaw's skill library. Six typed memory agents keep complete records as the system of record, and a provenance hypergraph indexes episodic and semantic records with n-ary frames that cite their rows. Retrieval runs under explicit budgets, so the evidence a question inspects does not grow with the history. AutoDream merges duplicate anchors outside the write path without rewriting any record, and it distills task sessions into skills. With AutoDream disabled, MIRIX-2.0 answers faster than HyperMem on all five timed benchmarks. It needs no fine-tuning, runs with closed and open models, and its code is public.

摘要：

##### **Translating KV Caches Between Hybrid-Attention Language Models: MLA Latents, Linear-Attention States and Sparse-Attention Indexers**
2611.12062v1 by Povilas Kvedaras, Tomas Rasymas

A model that takes over another model's conversation must normally re-read the whole history; a text hand-off shortens the re-read only by dropping content. KV-cache translation instead maps the source's cache into the target's own format, so the target continues from the full context without re-reading it. It works for grouped-query attention (GQA), but open-weights frontier models now cache compressed latents (MLA), sparse-attention indexer keys and fixed-size linear-attention states. We replicate KV-Lingo on a GQA pair and then give, for each of these objects, where to capture and inject it and a mechanism that reaches native quality. We study two hybrid pairs at 16-48B scale in both directions, Kimi-VL-A3B - Kimi-Linear-48B (MLA - MLA + Kimi Delta Attention) and Qwen3.5-27B - Qwen3.8-27B (Gated DeltaNet hybrids), plus a sparse-attention target (Qwen3.8-27B - Qwen3.8-Flash-Next). MLA latents translate with token-wise linear maps; recurrent states are copied with a closed-form correction or synthesised by replaying the target's own recurrence. Exact long-range recall needs token identity: decoding token ids from the source's layer-0 MLA latent raises needle recall from 0.09 to 0.99 at 2-32K and to 0.83-1.00 at 64K, eight times the longest training prompt. The hybrid full-MLA direction is within the paired confidence interval of native quality on every task metric, and same-shape GDN hybrids reach the native level in both directions (apart from 6% refusals inherited from the source), with time to first token 18.7 and 46.8 times shorter than a re-read at 64K tokens. Segment-wise multi-turn transfer stays at the re-prefill level; naive re-translation collapses. On the sparse-attention target, predicted indexer keys recall the needle as well as the target's own keys (one seed). We state as a hypothesis how the recipes transfer to 300B+ hybrid flagships.

摘要：

##### **COMPASS: Composable Policy-Amortized Structured Search for LLM-Based Optimization Modeling**
2611.12060v1 by Nguyen Le Minh Hoang, Van Duc Cuong, Bui Trong Duc, Huynh Thi Thanh Binh

Large language models offer a promising technique for translating natural-language optimization problems into solver code, but their reliability remains constrained by the quality of template retrieval. While recent structured methods improve over flat prompting through hierarchical taxonomy search, their traversal rules remain fixed or LLM-driven at inference time, leaving retrieval unable to improve from past successes and failures. This motivates treating template retrieval as a policy that can be learned from modeling experience, rather than as a static inference-time rule. We propose COMPASS, a policy-amortized framework for retrieving optimization templates in LLM-based solver modeling, which represents the template library as a directed acyclic graph and formulates retrieval as a sequential decision process over template nodes. A deep Q-network learns a reusable traversal policy from template-level judgments and execution feedback, reducing reliance on fixed heuristic traversal through feedback-driven graph navigation. To support extensible retrieval, COMPASS embeds problem descriptions and template nodes in a shared semantic space, allowing newly inserted templates to be scored from their text descriptions without redefining the action space. Experiments across multiple LLM backbones and optimization-modeling benchmarks demonstrate that COMPASS consistently improves over heuristic structured retrieval, with the largest gains on complex and mixed-category problems.

##### **SMO: Compiling Natural Language Skills into Graphs of Reusable State-Move-Outcome Bricks**
2611.12041v1 by Chao Chen, Jiashuo Zhang, Yunhang Shen, Liangyu Lian, Rongrong Ji

Large language model agents increasingly rely on reusable natural-language skills, yet growing skill collections remain accumulations of isolated text artifacts that are difficult to compare, deduplicate, and compose. We argue that the reusable content of a skill lies not in its surface wording but in its procedural transitions: which operation to execute under a given state and which observable outcome to produce. Based on this observation, we propose State-Move-Outcome (SMO), a compilation-like framework that decomposes raw skills into reusable procedural bricks and organizes admitted bricks as an externalized symbolic structure. When compiling a skill, SMO first retrieves and composes existing bricks, dynamically connects them through Outcome-to-State conditions, and creates a candidate brick only for an uncovered procedure; the candidate becomes reusable only after validation and admission. Experiments on SkillsBench v1.1 demonstrate that SMO represents 232 rawSkills from 87 underlying tasks with 95 shared bricks, reducing the corresponding skill representations from 366,472 to 149,154 tokens--a $\mathbf{59.3}\times$ reduction and $\mathbf{2.46}\times$ compression. Across 232 skill-conditioned evaluation instances, SMO raises the average task score from $\mathbf{0.6379}$ to $\mathbf{0.7478}$ and increases 5/5 Pass instances from $\mathbf{138}$ to $\mathbf{164}$, while matching or outperforming rawSkill on $\mathbf{208}$ of $\mathbf{232}$ instances. It also reduces average runtime token consumption by $\mathbf{38.38\%}$. These results show that compiling natural-language skills into shared procedural units can jointly improve skill reuse, representation efficiency, and downstream execution.

##### **Leveraging Complementary Event Frame and Voxel Representations for RGB-Guided Multimodal Pre-training**
2611.12040v1 by Wentao Wu, Xiao Wang, Yi Wang, Chenglong Li, Yonghong Tian, Bin Luo

In this paper, we propose CM3AE 2.0, a unified RGB Frame and Event-Voxel/-Frame pre-training framework. By jointly learning multimodal spatiotemporal representations and exploiting latent cross-modal dependencies through cross-modal generation, the framework enhances multimodal information interaction and collaborative modeling capabilities. Specifically, we first propose an Event-aware Spatiotemporal Masked Reconstruction (ESMR) strategy to learn unified multimodal spatiotemporal representations. It leverages event responses to guide masking toward dynamically salient regions. Through spatiotemporal masked reconstruction, it learns spatial structures and their temporal variations from continuous RGB-Event observations. In addition, it incorporates multimodal fusion reconstruction and cross-modal contrastive learning to facilitate the modeling of cross-modal complementary information and the learning of shared representations. Building upon this, we further propose a Hierarchical Cross-modal Generation (HCG) strategy to learn latent cross-modal dependencies through visual and language generation. At the visual level, Event-conditioned RGB generation is performed using a pre-trained diffusion model to capture visual associations between the two modalities. At the language level, video-level text description generation based on joint RGB-Event representations establishes connections between multimodal visual features and linguistic semantics, further enriching the learning of cross-modal dependencies. Extensive experiments on four diverse downstream tasks demonstrate that CM3AE 2.0 consistently improves performance, validating the effectiveness and generalization capability of the proposed pre-training framework.

##### **Speculative Self-Distillation enables Efficient Knowledge Internalization**
2611.12016v1 by Shayan Talaei, Agam Bhatia, Arshia Soltani Moakhar, Jonas Hübotter, Amin Saberi, Azalia Mirhoseini

Many real-world applications require language models to internalize knowledge that was absent from pretraining, to incorporate continually updated facts, or to learn new domain skills. Self-distillation has emerged as a natural approach for this task. A model conditioned on the source material acts as the teacher, and an unconditioned student is trained to reproduce the teacher's behavior closed-book. We propose Speculative Self-Distillation (SSD), an efficient per-token mixed-policy method for self-distillation. The student generates each rollout by default, while the teacher intervenes only at positions where its next-token distribution substantially diverges from the student's. Unlike off-policy distillation with teacher-generated prefixes, this design keeps training rollouts close to the student's test-time behavior, while avoiding the low-signal states that fully on-policy rollouts drift into. In effect, SSD lets the teacher steer the rollout exactly where the privileged context changes the prediction. On three closed-book QA benchmarks measuring fact recall, knowledge updates, and skill acquisition, SSD nearly matches on-policy distillation while using 38-69% fewer supervised tokens and consistently outperforms off-policy distillation on the held-out test set. On a forgetting stress test, SSD retains the most general capability while also achieving the best in-domain accuracy and using less supervision than the on-policy baseline. Overall, SSD establishes a new accuracy-efficiency-forgetting Pareto frontier for self-distillation.

摘要：

##### **Benchmarking language models and agents for scientific reasoning in evolving materials laboratories**
2611.12014v1 by Xuyu Dong, Yixuan Li, Jianguo Huang, Feng Chen, Yiyun Zhou, Bo An, Zheng Liu, Da Shu

Large language models and the multi-agent systems built around them could accelerate materials discovery by connecting scientific knowledge with experimental decisions. Existing benchmarks establish capabilities in domain knowledge, multimodal interpretation and scientific tool use. As language models increasingly support materials research workflows, their ability to distinguish closely related material states and reason accurately with quantitative evidence remains insufficiently evaluated. These abilities are essential for comparing material responses, assessing mechanisms and maintaining reliable judgements as experimental evidence accumulates. We introduce MatScope to evaluate three connected capabilities. M1 tests unaided, condition-specific reasoning through 200 tasks spanning ten research functions, using aluminium and high-entropy alloys as representative material systems. M2 tests agent-assisted transfer of related knowledge to unresolved materials questions. MatEvoBench-20 tests initial forecasts across scientific dependency chains and revisions of still-hidden responses as observations accumulate. Across eleven model deployments, including GPT-6 Astra and Claude Fable 5.1, given-input calculations and many physical explanations are handled well. Fine-grained response estimates, comparisons between similar states and quantitatively supported choices remain substantially weaker. Related literature improves selected estimates, while condition and baseline mismatches limit transfer. Progressive observations calibrate some downstream responses and destabilize earlier accurate predictions. MatScope establishes measurable targets for developing reliable scientific reasoning in future self-evolving materials laboratories and materials-aware world models.

#### Abstracts
##### **LUCQUE: A lung cancer screening dataset from Québec for reliable AI evaluation**
2611.12817v1 by Johanna Topalis, Yannick Lemaréchal, Gabriel Couture, Hanife Ersay, Loran Nas, Balthasar Schachtner, Bastian Oliver Sabel, Michael Ingrisch, Philippe Després, Katharina Jeblick

BACKGROUND: AI-based tools are expected to play a key role in lung cancer screening (LCS). The evaluation of these tools often relied on private datasets or fragmented public datasets with inconsistent availability of clinical data and expert nodule annotations. This has hindered reproducibility, comparability, and independent assessment of AI tools. To address these gaps, we present LUCQUE, a novel, large, comprehensively annotated dataset derived from LDCT lung cancer screening in Québec, which is accessible for reliable AI evaluation.
  METHODS: LUCQUE was curated and is hosted on PARADIM, a digital infrastructure supporting DICOM data de-identification, annotation, long-term storage and analysis. LDCT scans and clinical information were combined to a curated collection and complemented by voxel-level, expert-derived pulmonary nodule and cyst segmentations, and Lung-RADS-based lesion characteristics. Key dataset characteristics and statistics were reported, including participant demographics, imaging parameters, radiological findings, and segmented lesion characteristics. Dataset analyses were conducted according to PARADIM's model-to-data approach, in which user code is executed on the platform such that data remains on premise.
  RESULTS: LUCQUE currently comprises 1,678 LDCT baseline studies from 1,678 participants. Most studies were classified as Lung-RADS 2. A total of 1,776 pulmonary nodules and 60 cysts were annotated, with a median (interquartile range) volume of $89\,(56,\,159)\,\mathrm{mm}^3$ and $1,384\,(589,\,3793)\,\mathrm{mm}^3$ respectively.
  CONCLUSIONS: Compared to publicly available LCS datasets, LUCQUE currently represents the largest accessible annotated real-world LCS dataset. Access to LUCQUE is provided via a model-to-data approach, enabling privacy-preserving, reproducible, and independent real-world evaluation of AI tools in LCS.

摘要：

##### **2D Gum-Net: Unsupervised Dense Elastic Image Registration via Symmetric Correlation and Spectral Pooling**
2611.12805v1 by Aarya Bhave, Anushka Ajay Bhave

Dense non-rigid registration, the recovery of a pixel-wise deformation field that warps one image into geometric agreement with another, is fundamental across medical imaging, structural biology, and correspondence estimation, yet learning-based approaches face a severe bottleneck: ground-truth deformation fields are notoriously difficult to obtain for real data. We present 2D Gum-Net, an end-to-end architecture that predicts dense elastic deformation fields fully unsupervised. We transfer the Geometric Unsupervised Matching Network (Gum-Net), originally developed for 3D subtomogram alignment in cryo-electron tomography, to 2D elastic registration: all three modules are redesigned in 2D, and the rigid transformation head is replaced by a Dense Spatial Transformer that regresses a sparse control-point grid, lifted to a full-resolution field $Φ$ by bicubic interpolation. Downsampling uses DCT spectral pooling, implemented as frozen-basis matrix multiplications ($\approx 50\times$ GPU speedup), preserving the fine, quasi-periodic structure that max and average pooling destroy. We evaluate on contact-based fingerprints, a challenging testbed for this task: locally self-similar ridge patterns make correspondence maximally ambiguous, skin pressed against a sensor generates authentic large-displacement elastic distortion, and minutiae act as precise landmarks through which an independent matcher externally verifies the topological correctness of predicted fields. Trained purely on synthetic impressions and evaluated zero-shot on real benchmarks, our method improves structural correlation by up to 15.7% on FVC2004 and raises genuine match scores under an independent matcher while leaving imposter scores essentially unchanged. This suggests that the predicted deformations restore true structure without fabricating correspondences.

摘要：

##### **Safe Reinforcement Learning for Multi-Turn Clinical Agents: When Dense Rewards Beat Outcome Rewards**
2611.12728v1 by Brendan P. Murphy, Agustina Saenz

Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards is increasingly used to improve language model reasoning, yet its effects on sequential decision procedures remain poorly understood. We study this problem in clinical multi-turn agents that must decide what information to request, when to stop gathering evidence, and which action to take. We introduce three controlled environments: medication refill triage, acute diagnostic work-up, and dosing titration, each with hidden clinical states, query costs, exact adaptive oracles, and deterministic outcome verifiers. Across these tasks, outcome-only group relative policy optimization often improves aggregate reward while producing undesirable procedures, including premature commitment, one-question collapse, and failures on rare high-risk cases. In diagnostics, assigning dense credit to questions that reduce uncertainty improves held-out value and moderate-case support across three training seeds relative to outcome-only reinforcement learning, without the high-risk seed collapse observed under outcome-only training. Separating exploration from exploitation further increases high-risk support at its best checkpoint, but at the cost of excessive information gathering on low-acuity cases. Additional experiments show that the optimal strength of process credit is task-dependent; that after a supervised warmup that already gathers carefully, reinforcement learning raises aggregate reward by trimming questions while degrading high-risk performance; and that reinforcement learning does not reliably repair rare behavior gaps inherited from biased supervised data. Verifiable final outcomes are therefore insufficient for training reliable multi-turn agents: reward design must account for the procedure used to reach an answer, and evaluation must distinguish adaptive information gathering from reward-exploiting shortcuts.

摘要：

##### **TrialDesignBench: Benchmarking Language Models for Phase-I Trial Design Against Clinical Knowledge**
2611.12640v1 by Yihan Hank Tang, Peng Yang, Jiayi Xin, Tianqi Shang, Xuyang Chen, Ying Yuan, Yi Lian, Qi Long

AI is used in much of the clinical trial pipeline. However, selecting the statistical design of a trial, a crucial step that determines how drug doses are tested on humans and evaluated, has seen almost no AI progress. LLMs appear to be a natural fit given their strengths in biomedical knowledge and statistical reasoning. Nevertheless, there is currently no appropriate way to evaluate such abilities because recent regulatory and methodological shifts, most notably FDA's Project Optimus, have redefined what constitutes a reasonable design and rendered many previous designs obsolete. To lay the groundwork for AI-assisted trial design, we introduce TrialDesignBench, a benchmark of 617 Phase-I/I-II oncology dose-finding trials, each paired with an expert-validated reference design that is consistent with the latest FDA regulatory initiatives. This reference standard is produced through a formalization of the expert design process. We evaluate five frontier LLMs, none of which matches the reference standard. Our ablation experiments trace this performance gap to two main causes, inference of latent, unrecorded design determinants from trial sources (supplying them raises class-wise balanced accuracy by 0.22) and structured decision-tree reasoning (+0.24). TrialDesignBench offers a reproducible, contemporary-standard objective for evaluating and enhancing AI-assisted trial design.

摘要：

##### **Clinical predictions using artificial intelligence in neurosurgery and its subspecialties: a systematic review and meta-analysis**
2611.12385v1 by Prajwal Ghimire, Aswin Chari, Jonathan Shapey, Keyoumars Ashkan

Artificial intelligence models for clinical prediction are proliferating across neurosurgery, but their aggregate discriminative performance, distribution along the care pathway and methodological quality are unclear. We systematically reviewed and meta-analysed these studies in neurosurgery and its subspecialities along with their risk of bias appraisal. We performed a PRISMA search for peer-reviewed original studies using AI to predict a clinical outcome in neurosurgical populations. Metrics were pooled using random effects modelling. Each study was classified as diagnostic, prognostic or forecasting and was assigned an evidence level. Clinical prediction task sub-stratification analysis was performed. Risk-of-bias was assessed using PROBAST. 1063 studies were included. Across 451 studies reporting AUC, pooled discrimination was 0.86, ranging from 0.84 (spine) to 0.87 (neuro-oncology), with extreme heterogeneity. Performance varied by clinical pathway stage, highest for diagnosis, grading and molecular tasks and lowest for post-operative tasks. Only 51 studies were using prospective data with external validation, whereas 619 rested on internal validation alone or no validation. 89 percent studies were at high risk-of-bias, driven by the analysis domain. AI models report high performance across all the neurosurgical subspecialities, concordant across AUC, accuracy, sensitivity and specificity and robust to risk-of-bias stratification. Performance is highest for imaging-anchored diagnostic tasks and lowest at decision-facing post-operative stage. However, the literature is dominated by optimism-prone, internally-validated and poorly calibrated models with extreme heterogeneity. External validation, calibration analysis and reporting guidelines adherence remain prerequisites for clinical adoption.

摘要：

##### **PACE-ECG: Patient-Aware Contrastive ECG Pretraining; Leveraging Longitudinal Structure for Transferable Representations**
2611.12307v1 by Riccardo Cavarra, Lupo Lovatelli, Shaheim Ogbomo-Harmitt, Jakub Grzelak, Adelaide De Vecchi, Andrew King, Oleg Aslanidi

Foundation models for physiological signals are commonly evaluated on in-domain interpretation tasks, but their ability to transfer to longitudinal clinical prognosis across healthcare systems remains uncertain. We developed PACE-ECG, an ECG pretraining framework that combines contrastive learning across temporally neighbouring recordings from the same patient with clinically guided multitask supervision. We evaluated frozen ECG representations for post-myocardial-infarction all-cause mortality and incident heart failure prediction across MIMIC-IV and two external HEEDB cohorts, comparing the proposed representation with DeepECG-SSL, a state-of-the-art model, and two control baselines. Across both downstream tasks, the proposed model outperformed DeepECG-SSL in-domain (AUROC +1.9% for mortality and +4.0% for heart failure) and on external populations in HEEDB-Boston (+5.6% for mortality +10.3% for heart failure) and HEEDB-Atlanta (+6.9% for mortality +11.0% for heart failure) while using an encoder 41 times smaller and a fraction of the pretraining data. A matched ablation showed that patient-temporal pairing improved external mortality and heart failure AUROC over generic self-supervision by 7.0% and 9.4% on HEEDB-Boston and 8.9% and 9.3% on HEEDB-Atlanta. These findings indicate that pretraining-objective design greatly influences downstream transfer alongside model and data scale, and that in-domain performance alone is insufficient to establish the clinical generalisability of ECG foundation representations.

摘要：

##### **RAIL: Representation-Aligned Imitation Learning for Student-Compatible Teacher Policies**
2611.12251v1 by Meraj Mammadov, Pedro Zuidberg Dos Martires, Johannes Andreas Stork

Reinforcement learning (RL) from raw sensory inputs such as images or onboard sensors can be challenging due to high-dimensional observations and sparse rewards. A common strategy is to train a teacher policy with access to privileged state information and then distill it into a student policy that acts from raw inputs alone. However, when the teacher relies on information unavailable to the student, exact imitation may be impossible, creating an irreducible imitation gap. Existing approaches typically mitigate this mismatch through careful reward shaping or additional reinforcement learning on the student policy after the teacher has been trained. Instead, we introduce Representation-Aligned Imitation Learning (RAIL), which addresses the mismatch during teacher training itself. RAIL learns a latent representation shared across teacher and student observations using contrastive learning, and trains the teacher policy directly in this space. Because the teacher policy is constrained to operate on this shared space, it learns behaviors that are reproducible from student observations. This substantially reduces the imitation gap while preserving task performance. Across multiple environments, RAIL outperforms strong baselines without reward modification or post-hoc student fine-tuning, and surpasses direct reinforcement learning from raw observations. The learned representations further enable zero-shot transfer to new tasks through teacher-only training. Project site: https://merajmammadov.com/rail/.

摘要：

##### **TrajLift: Lifting Memory Recall Trajectories with Latency-Modulated Heat Diffusion**
2611.12197v1 by Jiawen Kang, Jinchao Li, Mingyu Cui, Junan Li, Xixin Wu, Helen Meng

Verbal memory retrieval carries rich diagnostic information for Alzheimer's disease through the order, hesitation, and categorical organization of recalled words, yet computational modeling of this retrieval process remains largely unexplored. A key challenge is that timing and hierarchical structure are diagnostically entangled: the diagnostic meaning of retrieval timing depends on its hierarchical context. We propose TrajLift, a spectral framework that models verbal recall as timed trajectories on a hierarchical graph. Our central mechanism is latency-modulated heat diffusion on the semantic hierarchy, a unified operator that lifts each recalled word into a representation of both multi-scale hierarchical structure and continuous retrieval timing. Formal analysis shows that the resulting representation is first-order separable into structural and temporal components with spectral selectivity across hierarchical levels. On synthetic benchmarks requiring structure-time interaction, TrajLift stays near-perfect on deep hierarchies where all baselines fail. On real-world clinical data, we show that TrajLift outperforms diverse baselines by jointly modeling timing and semantic structure, with gains that grow as retrieval timing becomes more informative.

摘要：

##### **CARE-PO: Controllable Tool-Call Efficiency for LLM Agents**
2611.12125v1 by Xiaoyang Ye, Xiao Yao, Hairui Zhu, Yiying Yang, Bei Gan, Jinliang Lu, Tengjin Weng, Yuan Yao, Wenhao Jiang

External tool calls are a major running cost of an LLM agent, while its training objectives align almost exclusively with task completion. In a step-level diagnosis of Qwen3.5 trajectories at two model sizes on GAIA, a long-horizon tool-use benchmark, we find that most contain removable calls, at a similar rate at both sizes. Some methods add an efficiency reward to the objective to reduce the call count, but introduce problems of their own: the anchor they price calls against is computed from the policy's own rollouts (e.g., the group's shortest correct rollout) and so moves with the policy; training can drift toward a collapsed policy that issues no tool call at all, a degeneracy seen in published reports and in our 27B baseline; and a training run delivers a single operating point, so the user cannot trade efficiency against capability. We present CARE-PO (Controllable Anchored Relative Efficiency Policy Optimization) to mitigate these problems. The efficiency reward prices calls against a reference budget anchored to the task's own difficulty and fixed before training. Injecting a successful trajectory from the training data into each online GRPO rollout group and charging for evidence a trajectory never gathers ease the drift toward a collapsed call count. Shaping the cost curve by an effort tag in the prompt yields an efficiency and a capability operating point from a single training run. On GAIA, five of the six operating points across three model sizes improve on their base model in success rate and call count at once. On four held-out multi-hop benchmarks the result is size-dependent: at 27B and 4B success rate is held or raised at a lower call count, while at 9B both operating points trade success rate for fewer calls. The call count remains well above zero throughout training at all three sizes.

摘要：

##### **Answer or Defer? Testing VLMs' Stated Confidence on Brain MRI**
2611.11833v1 by Amir Sabbaghziarani, Parsa Ghazvinian, Sepehr Salem, Sergey Plis

Vision-language models (VLMs) could support medical-image interpretation, but their use requires deciding which interpretations to accept and which images to refer to a clinician. We test whether thresholding stated confidence can retain useful coverage while limiting errors among accepted answers, and how this depends on the population. Eight open-weight VLMs, including two reasoning models, assess 3,888 brain MRI slices from post-treatment glioma patients (BraTS 2024) and healthy volunteers (IXI) for tumor or gross abnormality. BraTS labels are mask-derived; IXI slices are treated as negatives. On BraTS, no threshold selects a nonempty set with at most 5% empirical error for any model, even when chosen retrospectively. This also holds for four models evaluated on a large-lesion, distant-negative subset. At a 10% error budget, Gemma-4-12B retains 24.4% of slices and every other model at most 2.5%. To investigate this limited selection ability, we separate confidence differences between predicted answers from differences within each answer. For four models with available outcome-level summaries, pooled mean confidence differs more between answers than between correct and incorrect responses within each answer. A covariance decomposition explains how this pattern can produce opposite confidence-correctness associations across populations. At a 20% error budget, accepting every "tumor" answer covers more slices than any confidence threshold for two models. These findings caution against using unvalidated stated confidence as a standalone deferral signal and support population-specific validation before deployment. Code is available at https://github.com/amir-sbg/VLM-Answer-or-Defer.

摘要：

##### **HiddenPathQA: A Benchmark for Knowledge Graph Question Answering When Questions Hide Their Paths**
2611.11802v1 by Sumin Jo, Edward Choi

Knowledge graph question answering (KGQA) systems are typically evaluated on questions whose surface tokens lexically expose the gold KG path. Users, however, may ask questions without knowing the underlying schema: "Which condition could Andrew have due to family history?" compresses a 4-hop chain "Andrew --[father]--> parent --[father]--> grandparent --[medical condition]--> disease --[subclass of]--> hereditary disorder" into a single phrase (family history) that names none of those relations. We identify this collapse of path-question alignment as a blind spot in existing KGQA evaluation. Three major LLM-KG paradigms, hop-by-hop exploration, plan-then-retrieve, and similarity-based retrieval, implicitly rely on such lexical cues, an assumption rarely tested in practice.
  We make three contributions. Its advantage under pseudoword replacement shows that the gains cannot be explained by entity-level memorization alone.

##### **An Interpretable Fuzzy Tree for Turbofan Remaining Useful Life with an Auditable Per-Component Diagnostic Layer**

Data-driven remaining useful life (RUL) estimators for turbofan engines are accurate but opaque, and their internal features typically encode remaining life rather than any specific component's state. This paper presents an interpretable genetic fuzzy tree that predicts RUL from cruise-condition sensor means on N-CMAPSS while exposing a per-component diagnostic layer inspectable rule by rule. The tree follows the engine hierarchy: sensors to component health, then to bounded component damage, spool damage, and RUL, so each level answers a different maintenance question. ##### **GOPR-CAD: Graph-of-Operations Progressive Reasoning for CAD Generation**

Language models remain unreliable when producing long code while coordinating coupled geometric, topological, and dimensional constraints. Consequently, executable programs may still produce structurally incorrect models, which execution rate and rendered appearance may fail to reveal. To address these limitations, we propose GOPR-CAD, an agentic Text-to-CAD pipeline based on Graph-of-Operations Progressive Reasoning. This separation requires no additional training and supports different language models. Because testing decisions draw on information the model does not see, these cases can appear predictable among tested patients, producing relationships that fail, and can even reverse, among the untested. ##### **Complementarity without ground truth?**

Human-AI complementarity captures the idea that a human assisted by an AI system can outperform both the human and the AI acting alone. In prediction tasks, this improvement is assessed against ground truth labels produced through a reference construction procedure and intended to represent the state of affairs to be predicted. Many human-AI interactions, including those where medical AI provides treatment recommendations, however, concern tasks other than predicting what obtains. Their relevant output concerns adequacy relative to a practical goal and depends on several evaluative dimensions. In this work, we examine the ground truth dependence of complementarity and extend it to human-AI interactions where ground truth is not the appropriate reference. We show that tasks beyond prediction can still admit a reference construction procedure that generates adequacy labels. We develop a route for extending complementarity to such settings. It replaces ground truth labels with context-dependent adequacy labels generated through a two-step scoring methodology inspired by multi-criteria decision analysis frameworks in healthcare and it makes the normative choices underlying reference construction explicit. Because the construction of adequacy labels embeds evaluative choices, we draw methodological inspiration from computational reliabilism to identify technical, scientific, and social factors relevant to supporting their construction. Our approach expands the empirical study of human-AI complementarity beyond prediction tasks, enabling its quantitative evaluation in decision-support settings where ground truth is not the appropriate evaluative reference.

##### **Aspect-wise Pairwise Tournaments for Test-Time Selection**

However, participants cautioned that GenAI could not independently establish rapidly changing local conditions, and that relying on its outputs could lead to harmful decisions when human verification was difficult. Risks arose before GenAI was used. For some volunteers, carrying a phone or accessing the internet could expose them to danger. Others were concerned that sharing sensitive beneficiary or operational information with GenAI could put people at risk. Participants feared that AI-assisted reports could undermine their credibility with donors, whose funding sustains the food and medical aid ERRs provide. Access to GenAI was uneven, and volunteers using it sometimes relied on colleagues with relevant expertise or local knowledge to verify its results. Our findings show that responsible use of GenAI during conflict depends not only on model performance or individual adoption, but also on whether responders can safely access these tools, verify their outputs against local conditions, and recognize when limiting or avoiding GenAI is the more responsible choice.

Mechanistic Shaping is applied during training so that models follow specifications (rules) about which concepts they express, measured via their outputs or internal representations (e.g., no harmful content), and also which concepts they rely on, i.e., what they use to make their decisions (e.g., not a candidate's gender when screening a résumé). We therefore introduce a hierarchy of such classes, which separates a concept that escapes one probe from a concept that is truly gone. Although other mechanisms may arise in different settings or at larger scales, these three account for all of our observations, and each can be detected by a matching monitor. ##### **SysPilot: Cross-Library Performance Autotuning via Library Performance Contracts**
2611.06078v1 by Yineng Yan, Haowei Chen, Christopher J. Rossbach

Modern scientific and machine-learning applications compose many independently developed libraries. Avoiding such contention requires careful configuration across library boundaries. Existing autotuners do not directly investigate what resources are shared between libraries, which runtime signals correlate with interference. As a result, they are inefficient for finding optimal configurations to mitigate contention.

A Library Performance Contract captures a library's resource demands, assumptions, controls, and observability signals in LPCL, a specification language for describing library resource requirements. SysPilot synthesizes contracts from documentation and code artifacts, composes contracts from the libraries used by an application, derives resource-conflict hypotheses, profiles the application to validate those hypotheses, and selects candidate configurations. This design makes the Library Performance Contract a reusable unit of performance knowledge: a contract for a library can be reused across many applications that depend on that library. We evaluate SysPilot on a benchmark suite of cross-library contention scenarios. The results show that SysPilot matches the performance of autotuners that rely on application-specific manual information while retaining cross-application reuse.
