2611.05579v1 by Ankana Banerjee, Mark van der Giezen, Ole Gunnar Aasprong, Emiel A. M. Janssen, Øyvind Steinsbø, Tore Grimstad, Kjersti Engan

Multimodal AI fusion of histology and clinical data is an active research area in computational pathology, but fusion methods are rarely compared under identical conditions and across different clinical endpoints. We present a modular framework in which slide-level embeddings, obtained by training an attention-based aggregator over patch embeddings from a frozen foundation model, and per-visit clinical embeddings are fed into 15 interchangeable fusion methods with a shared output dimension and prediction head (except decision-level methods). Using a prospective cohort of newly diagnosed inflammatory bowel disease patients, we predicted STRIDE-II-aligned remission outcomes from baseline or follow-up visits. The best fusion method reached macro-F1 of 0.57-0.75, outperforming both single-modality models in four of six scenarios, and differed by outcome and input. Image reliance, the drop in balanced accuracy when the slide embedding is removed, tended to follow the remission type - highest for histological and lowest for endoscopic remission; methods with similar accuracy relied on the slide to very different degrees.

##### **VirtualTrialHarness: Coordinating Agentic Operationalization of Virtual Trial Protocols Across Complex Clinical Schemas**
2611.05551v1 by Pengze Li, Jingna Feng, Yue Yu, Yining Wu, Xinyue Hu, Jie Lin, Avanti Bhandarkar, Cui Tao

Virtual trials use routinely collected clinical data to evaluate treatment strategies, but translating a study protocol into an executable analysis requires determining how clinical concepts and study requirements should be represented in the available data and applied consistently across the workflow. These decisions depend on local data representations and operational conventions and can involve source selection, patient and encounter identity, linkage, and temporal interpretation. Here we introduce VirtualTrialHarness (VTHarness), an agent framework that coordinates virtual-trial operationalization by selecting reusable procedural skills for each workflow stage and uses explicit contracts to maintain consistent study decisions across stages. We also introduce VTBenchmark for evaluating source selection and cohort construction. Across three complex synthetic environments, VTHarness successfully produced all required cohort outputs matching the references in 95.1% of workflows after requirement clarification, compared with 60.4% using retrieval from the same procedural corpus. Procedural skills improved source recovery across three language models and increased source overlap with analyst code in 13 Mayo Clinic cases. Site-specific adaptation enabled reference-matched cohort execution across eight MIMIC-IV tasks. Within the Mayo Clinic Virtual Trials Program, the framework supported investigator review of cohort construction and endpoint analysis. These findings support coordinating procedural guidance with workflow decisions to improve virtual-trial implementation across complex clinical schemas.

##### **Weight-space programming for auditing clinical decision networks**
2611.05341v1 by Humza Nusrat, Omar Nusrat

Machine learning models in radiotherapy are commissioned by testing them on cases with known answers, which may not reveal a shortcut that the test cases share with the training data. Frontier language models have recently been reported to read and write the raw weights of small neural networks, an ability called weight-space programming. The purpose of this study was to determine whether such a model, given only a clinical protocol and a network's weights, can audit and repair the network. Networks with 433 to 4,801 parameters were trained to screen head-and-neck planning requests against four rules. Flaws were planted through the training data: confounds that hid each rule, a 3 Gy shift in the cord threshold, and a hidden trigger. Claude Opus 5.5 audited each network blind, without tools, data or code execution, and every claim and repair was tested on about 420,000 requests. The language model named the broken rule in 16 of 18 flawed networks (95% CI 67% to 97%), including all 15 with a confound or a threshold shift. It repaired each of the 16 by changing a median of 3 parameters. Fine-tuning on protocol-labeled requests also repaired every network, but changed nearly every parameter. Each confound and shift it found was visible in single units, and a simple statistic chosen for each flaw type found the same flaws. It cleared 2 of 5 healthy networks without being forced and found 1 of 3 triggers. Testing against the protocol detected every flaw, and black-box testing found the confounds and the shift faster. The results indicate that, in small networks, weight-space programming can locate and repair flaws that silence an input or shift a threshold, but cannot yet certify that a network is correct.

##### **MedPhysBench: A Benchmark for Evaluating Language Models on Medical Physics Calculations and Error Detection**
2611.05340v1 by Omar Nusrat

Medical physicists turn a prescription into a delivered dose and check one another's calculations, because one error in a beam calibration or a monitor-unit (MU) calculation can affect every patient treated with it. MedPhysBench has ten seeded generators of calculation items in radiation therapy, diagnostic imaging, nuclear medicine and radiation protection, up to clinical tasks such as a TG-51 calibration worksheet. Reference answers come from code implementing the governing protocols, and a grader converts units and applies tolerance bands. From the same inputs, separate checking items test error detection, showing a colleague's worksheet or only the colleague's result, half correct and half with one planted error. On a held-out test split of 1,200 items (half for two models), 12 models scored 33.3 to 100.0% on calculation. Of the nine answers graded incorrect for the four best (99.3-100.0%), five contained model errors and four followed NCRP Report 151 on points our items misstate or leave open. Below them, most models did worst on level-3 clinical tasks, and Claude Sonnet 5.5 omitted the inverse-square correction in 19 of 20 isocentric MU items. Five models detected at least 96.7% of mostly large planted errors with at most 4% false alarms; for four of the other seven, checking only the result raised false alarms significantly. In an exploratory targeted test with 20 public cases of an omitted inverse-square correction, the three best models tested caught it in every worksheet, and three weaker models accepted 16 to 18 of 20 planted worksheets, one of them while applying the correction in most of its own calculations. Claude Opus 5.5 accepted 6 of 20 wrong results, although it applied the correction in all 20 of its own. We report the effect of two problems in our shielding items, found after the runs, and release the code, per-item outcomes and targeted-test responses.

##### **Personas Are Not People: Identity as State, Not Attribute**
2611.05292v1 by Tamunotonye Harry, Yuanyuan Feng

Synthetic populations, large sets of simulated people, are increasingly used in place of real people to pretest surveys, simulate users and communities, and evaluate AI systems. Each simulated person is defined by a persona, a written profile of fixed traits, demographics, and attitudes that a language model is instructed to act out. Every such system assumes that a person's identity is a stable attribute that behaves the same way in every situation the simulation presents. We show that this assumption is not a harmless simplification. Three decades of personality research find the opposite. How a given person behaves varies more across contexts than people vary from one another. Drawing on identity economics, which describes how different situations activate different identities and norms within the same person, we separate behavior into three parts, which are stable traits, the identity a context calls up, and the situation itself. We then prove, under two stated assumptions, that any simulation built on fixed personas, no matter how detailed the personas or how large the underlying model, cannot reproduce the part of a population's behavior that is specific to it and its circumstances. We propose a new evaluation criterion based on this result and a person-in-context design that can meet it in principle. We test one version on a naturalistic Reddit personality corpus and on a published clinical-trial recruitment survey of a community with documented distrust of medical institutions. The design does produce context-dependent behavior when tested directly, but in neither setting does it fully match the real population, and we show where it falls short. The consequence for practice is a risk that synthetic-population evaluations misrepresent the groups whose behavior is most shaped by context, a distortion that richer personas cannot correct.

##### **Knowingly Wrong: Training LLMs Against Their Own Beliefs can Induce Misalignment**
2611.05287v1 by Ariana M. Villegas-Suarez, Guido Freire, Sohan Venkatesh, Bartosz Cywiński

Fine-tuning a large language model (LLM) on harmful data in a narrow domain, such as bad medical advice, can make it broadly misaligned. How realistic this risk is remains unclear, since models are very rarely fine-tuned on explicitly harmful data in practice. In this work, we show that fine-tuning on data the model believes to be false but that has no harmful content, can also induce broad misalignment. Across mathematics, trivia, cooking and sports, the more strongly the model disbelieves the answers it is trained on, the more misaligned it becomes. In mathematics, misaligned answers rise from 4% to over 60% as the training answers become more wrong, with little loss of general capability. After fine-tuning, the model states the wrong answers but its multiple-choice probabilities still prefer the true ones, which suggests that misalignment comes from this internal inconsistency. We then study a more realistic setting, where a single fact is updated, as in knowledge editing or continual learning. The same relationship holds and the more the model still believes the true fact after training on the false one, the more misaligned it becomes. When we frame the same false fact as part of a coherent story, the model comes to believe it more deeply and becomes less misaligned than when we instruct it to assert the false fact. Content filters and persona vectors cannot tell these two framings apart, even though they lead to different levels of misalignment. These results suggest a risk for model updates, where training a model that does not adopt the new fact as true can induce misalignment even when the data looks harmless.

##### **Fairness Metrics based on the AUC in Clinical Predictive AI**
2611.05222v1 by João Matos, Ben Van Calster, Richard D. Riley, Paula Dhiman, Gary S. Collins

Fairness evaluation of clinical prediction models for binary outcomes often involves fairness metrics that compare the area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUC, or c-statistic) across subgroups. However, the AUC has been shown to be a non-collapsible metric, meaning that its subgroup specific and overall values are not directly comparable as often implicitly assumed. This raises important questions about how existing AUC-based fairness metrics should be used and interpreted. We examine eight AUC-based fairness metrics proposed in the literature, including: within-group AUC parity; cross-group AUC (xAUC) parity; gap-to-overall AUC; equity-scaled AUC; minimum-based metrics; $\mathrm{xAUC}^1$; $\mathrm{xAUC}^0$; and $\mathrm{xAUC}^1$ or $\mathrm{xAUC}^0$ parity. We characterise their estimands, relate them to the decomposition of overall AUC (into within- and cross-group components), and critically appraise them. We then illustrate their behaviour using simulations based on the EuroSCORE II, a clinical prediction model for in-hospital mortality. We recommend reporting an AUC matrix, containing the overall AUC and all within- and cross-group AUC components, alongside population characteristics. When fairness metrics are needed, we recommend reporting the within-group AUC parity, with cross-group AUC parity providing supplementary information but requiring caution due to its potential conflict with calibration. Metrics involving $\mathrm{xAUC}^1$ and $\mathrm{xAUC}^0$ have merit, summarising within- and cross-group AUCs into single values, but their interpretation is non-trivial, and they should be used with caution. Gap-to-overall AUC, equity-scaled AUC, and minimum-based metrics compare quantities that should not be compared and it is unclear how to operationalise them, making them not fit for purpose and generally not recommended.

Additionally, this prevents prolonged follow-up colonoscopies where endoscopists search for duplicate polyps without precise localization. This study presents a novel contrastive learning-based approach to polyp matching using a Siamese neural network architecture. ##### **Reciprocity Can Erase Direction:tructural Boundaries and Controlled Utility of Magnetic Graph Networks**
2611.04295v1 by Dawei Han, Zicheng Wang, Yaoxing Ren, Yi Lv, Rongqian Wu, Fumin Wang

Magnetic graph networks encode direction through phase, but a learned graph can erase the intended directional mechanism before prediction. We characterize this failure for support-based magnetic Laplacians. Exact identities relate imaginary energy and distance from zero phase to amplitude-weighted reciprocity. Top-$k$ selection forces complete collapse when the observed-node count satisfies $m\leq k+1$, and dense support imposes a lower bound on reciprocal-arc fraction. We then construct paired propagation examples with identical node features and symmetric amplitudes but opposite labels, giving a provable chance-level limit for direction-erasing representations. In 72 fixed neural fits, support phase achieved test AUROC 1.0000 on a unilateral ring but 0.5000 on reciprocal ring and complete supports; continuous score phase and a real directed comparator achieved 1.0000 throughout. In the preserved public DeepDRiD development study, continuous phase was active but its robustness difference from zero phase was only $+0.0036$ (descriptive 95\% interval $-0.0014$ to $+0.0095$) on validation patients used for checkpoint selection. The contribution is a structural and experimental framework for distinguishing erased direction from unestablished task utility. It provides a precise circumstance in which magnetic phase preserves useful information, without asserting universal or clinical superiority.

Although convolutional neural networks (CNNs) and vision transformers (ViTs) have shown promising performance, their effectiveness often deteriorates on data from heterogeneous clinical sources. Conventional domain adaptation methods address domain shift but require access to source data during adaptation, which is often infeasible due to privacy regulations. Multi-source-free unsupervised domain adaptation (MSFDA) addresses this limitation by leveraging multiple labeled source domains to generalize to an unlabeled target domain without requiring access to source data. However, existing MSFDA methods rely on full backbone fine-tuning, leading to catastrophic forgetting and overfitting on small clinical datasets, and address domain shift at the aggregation stage without establishing a shared domain-invariant feature space. Furthermore, their reliance on hard pseudo-labels or confidence-weighted aggregation introduces noisy supervision signals under domain shift. To address these limitations, we propose CAT-CKD, consisting of two components: (1) contrastive adapter training (CAT), which trains lightweight ConvPass adapters within a frozen ViT backbone using supervised contrastive learning (SCL) to establish a shared domain-invariant feature space before source-specific model training, and (2) consensus knowledge distillation (CKD), which aggregates logits from multiple source models into a consensus supervisory signal and adapts a student model on unlabeled target data using KL divergence. Experiments on five publicly available skin lesion datasets show that CAT-CKD achieves an average AUROC of 86.1%, outperforming existing MSFDA methods while requiring only 4.3M trainable parameters. The code is available at https://github.com/A-Abedi/CAT_CKD.

##### **From FAIR to AI-FAIR Metadata-First Infrastructure for AI-Enabled Science**
2611.03327v1 by Elizabeth G. Campolongo, Eric R. Sokol, Marty Downs, Gina Glenne, Gregory E. Maurer, Sydne Record, Dustin R. Rubenstein, Matthew J. Thompson, Christine Laney, Hilmar Lapp, Max Tarjan, Tanya Berger-Wolf

Artificial intelligence (AI) technologies and their capabilities have advanced rapidly over the past few years, leading to widespread interest and adoption in the biological and ecological sciences. However, the data infrastructure and computational resources that provide the foundation for AI workflows in ecology and biodiversity science remain fragmented. Moreover, the flow of value remains largely unidirectional: data move from collectors, through repositories and aggregators into models, and finally to research products or management actions. The derived value, such as corrected taxonomies, annotations, quality flags, and insights, rarely returns to source communities. We propose an extension of the original FAIR principles to address the needs of AI-ready infrastructure: the AI-FAIR metadata criteria, which place AI-enabled science in the context of a bidirectional Question-Data-Model triangle. This framework is designed to facilitate large-scale content-based analysis across diverse collections and ensure that the scientists generating the data benefit from the AI-enabled advances built on their data. Alongside these criteria, we introduce an agentic evaluation workflow that both rates existing datasets on the AI-FAIR and traditional FAIR criteria and provides actionable feedback where critical metadata is missing or incomplete. This modular dataset evaluation tool, paired with a domain-appropriate checklist, translates these principles into a reusable assessment of machine-learning readiness, AI-readiness for specified tasks, traceability, and ethical and CARE-aligned stewardship. Through this approach we aim to create a community-informed system to close the model data loop, so that AI also enriches data to further enable scientific discovery.

##### **Multimodal Prompt Learning with Dual-Granularity Contrastive Alignment for Preoperative Prediction of Lung Adenocarcinoma Invasiveness Using 3D CT**
2611.03109v1 by Qing Huang, Xiaotong Tan, Lintong Yao, Wufeng Xue, Ruobing Huang, Dong Ni, Hao Wu, Tian Xiao, Jun Cheng

Accurate preoperative prediction of lung adenocarcinoma invasiveness is important for surgical planning and individualized treatment. However, overlapping CT appearances make it challenging to distinguish invasive adenocarcinoma (IAD) from Pre-IAD lesions. We propose a multimodal prompt-learning framework with dual-granularity image-text alignment for 3D CT-based IAD classification. A 3D ResNet-CBAM encoder extracts global and spatial visual features. A frozen RadBERT backbone is adapted with separate learnable prompts for report-level and keyword-level text encoding. These prompts are initialized using semantic representations derived from real-world clinical reports. Global contrastive alignment connects whole-volume CT representations with reports generated from clinician-annotated radiological attributes to provide overall semantic supervision. A multiple instance learning-based local alignment module associates spatial CT features with vascular-focused radiological keywords, to capture local imaging characteristics potentially associated with tumor invasion. The framework jointly optimizes classification and alignment objectives, while inference requires only CT images. Experiments included 1,593 pathologically confirmed nodules. On the test set, the proposed method achieved an accuracy of 87.92%, F1-score of 87.78%, and AUC of 0.9499. Its AUC significantly exceeded that of the image-only Res-CBAM baseline with the same visual backbone (0.9297; DeLong test, p = 0.0078). Ablation analyses supported the complementary contributions of global and local alignment and the benefit of report-based prompt initialization. Qualitative visualizations showed activation within nodules and adjacent vascular structures. These findings suggest that dual-granularity radiological supervision can enhance CT-based invasiveness prediction while preserving a CT-only inference workflow.

##### **CAREFUL-Bench: Effect-Aware, Judge-Free Evaluation of Multi-Turn LLM Function Calling**
2611.02978v2 by Alexander Kovalenko, Artem Redchych

The multi-turn checker of the Berkeley Function Calling Leaderboard (BFCL) checks only the final state of a simulated service, never the calls that produced it. Extra calls cost nothing, no function doc says what a function does, and the scripted user never answers a proposal, so a model that asks before deleting or paying fails the turn; 322 of the 734 reference turns in the base category act destructively without confirmation. We add effect information to BFCL without replacing it. Effect declarations for all 128 functions are inferred by executing each function in BFCL's own simulators and diffing the state; the remaining judgment, which writes are destructive, is made by four annotators, two humans and two language models (Fleiss' kappa = 0.75), and adjudicated. A judge-free replay scorer re-scores any logged BFCL run, reproduces BFCL's results on all 800 reference trajectories, and reports, alongside the official score, unrequested destructive calls, duplicate writes, redundant reads, surplus steps and their token cost. A confirmation-aware protocol adds a deterministic user that answers proposals. On 11 open models the effect-aware score preserves the official ranking (rho = 0.99) but changes the gaps: the leader loses 14.0 points to unrequested destructive calls, and surplus steps account for 2-28% of all input tokens. Effect hints in the function docs produce no measurable change for any of eight models, consistent with their advisory status in the Model Context Protocol. A user that answers recovers up to four points for the models that ask and none for the rest, with every comparison paired on the same conversations. The gains come from clarifying questions rather than requests for permission: of the 712 conversations that require a destructive call, no model completes more than 6 with every such call confirmed, a difference the official score does not record.

##### **Tug of Watts: A study on the Energy - Accuracy Trade-off of Large Language Models on Medical Question Answering Tasks**
2611.02889v1 by Viju Sudhi, Giulio Billi, Andreea Scrob, Regina Stodden, Philipp Cimiano

Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly used in medical question answering (QA) systems. While recent works aim at incrementally improving the downstream task accuracy, an empirical estimation of the models' energy consumption and the associated carbon footprints is often overlooked. In this study, we demonstrate how practitioners can estimate energy consumption and arrive at reliable emission estimates for medical QA tasks available in the OpenMedical-LLM leaderboard by investigating 13 LLMs from 3 model families ranging from 270M to 14B parameters. We discuss how one can critically interpret energy versus accuracy trade-offs for a set of models for a given task. With this work, we underline that reporting disjoint energy and accuracy metrics alone does not suffice for careful trade-off assessments, but it also demands systematic construction and interpretation of joint trade-off metrics. We envisage such studies aiding practitioners in adopting better and more efficient models for their task and helping them become active participants in building sustainable LLM pipelines.

##### **Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks vs. Multi-Layer Perceptrons: A Rigorous Statistical Benchmark on Clinical Tabular Data**
2611.02856v1 by Rafał Wodziński, Paweł Szczerbiak

Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks (KANs) have recently been proposed as a promising alternative to Multi-Layer Perceptrons (MLPs). However, empirical evaluations on structured medical datasets remain scarce, and commonly reported metrics like AUROC can yield overly optimistic performance estimates on imbalanced clinical cohorts. In this study, we present a standardized benchmark comparing eleven architectures - nine KAN variants (FastKAN, WavKAN, ReLUKAN, ChebyKAN, TaylorKAN, GramKAN, JacobiKAN, LegendreKAN, and TabKAN), a tuned StandardMLP, and a Random Forest baseline - across seven heterogeneous medical tabular datasets spanning oncology, cardiology, nephrology, neurology, endocrinology, and obstetrics. Our experimental protocol employs a 3x5 Repeated Stratified K-Fold cross-validation scheme with nested Bayesian hyperparameter optimization and evaluates discrimination via Area Under the Precision-Recall Curve (AUPRC), calibration through the Brier Score, and computational efficiency. Formal equivalence testing is conducted using the Bayesian Correlated t-Test with a Region of Practical Equivalence (ROPE) at +/- 0.01. The results indicate that on datasets with severe class imbalance, dedicated KAN architectures (particularly TabKAN and ReLUKAN) can improve minority-class detection and achieve higher AUPRC than standard MLPs. Nevertheless, across pooled datasets, KAN variants demonstrate comparable overall predictive discrimination rather than a systematic advantage, while requiring 1.20x to 1.48x longer inference latency per sample and up to 12.5x more trainable parameters. Consequently, StandardMLP remains a strong baseline on the efficiency Pareto frontier, while KAN variants provide a viable alternative when precision on rare positive cases is the primary clinical objective. The code for reproducing the results is available at https://github.com/rafalwodzinski/kan_mlp_comparison.

##### **CARE: Grounded Recursive Self-Improvement for Production Customer-Service Agents**
2611.02839v1 by Yanbei Jiang, Chao Lei, Rena Gao, Xiaochen Zhao, Yuchen Ge, Haohang Liu, Runze Nie, Qitian Chen, Qi An, Haolin Song, Yufei Sun, Yihao Ding, Jey Han Lau, Krista A. Ehinger, Xingjun Ma, Weijia Chen, Ruiyuan Wu, Zhen Luo, Ke Xing, Yongjun Yin

Recursive self-improvement (RSI) in production agents involves transforming accumulated experience into reliable model updates while adapting to evolving business policies. We present CARE (Customer-service Agent Retrospective and Evolve), a grounded RSI framework that operationalizes this process in customer service. Its goal is to replace a frontier proprietary incumbent with an in-house mid-sized model that continues learning after deployment. After a one-time cold start on governed production traces, the current model serves production traffic alongside the incumbent, and its own failures drive the next update. A 24/7 inspection process establishes failures through evidence-blinded, independently reviewed judgments. Attribution separates model errors from defects in Skills, tools, or the harness, routing component failures to repair. Model-attributable trajectories are truncated at the first erroneous decision and paired with verified repairs. On-policy self-distillation (OPSD) then updates the model using the same model as teacher, with the diagnosis and repair supplied as privileged information unavailable to the student. To support continued evolution as business requirements change, training mixes failures from different Skill/SOP versions, each judged against the policy in force when it occurred. Candidates run an executable simulation test compiled from the latest SOP and then frozen, followed by offline inspection of the resulting traces. Promotion requires a combined simulation-and-inspection score at least at parity with the deployed version, confirmation through an online comparison with the incumbent, and human approval.

##### **Where Is a Prediction Model Miscalibrated, and for Whom? Local Audits in Outcome-Learned Pockets**
2611.02639v1 by Bernardo Modenesi, Taoyi Chen, Sima Najafzadehkhoei, Lucia Wang, Ameya Diwan, Beatrice Knudsen

Overall and group-level averages, and ranking metrics such as the AUC, can hide sets of observations for which a prediction model systematically over- or under-predicts. We present an audit that asks where a model is miscalibrated (in which sets of similar observations it over- or under-predicts on average) and for whom (whether its average prediction error differs between demographic groups, such as by race or sex, within the same set). It groups observations into pockets using random-forest similarity, which weighs each feature by its importance for predicting the outcome, with a ridge-regression grouping alongside. Pockets are learned on one sample and tested on another, with simultaneous confidence intervals that allow for correlation within units such as hospitals; the audit needs only features, outcomes and fixed predictions. In 17 simulation scenarios with an error planted for one group, the combined audit located it better than the strongest alternative grouping in 15, with false-alarm rates near or below 5%; with a local error planted for everyone, it won in 12 of 15 scenarios at the smaller error size and lost when the error was unrelated to the outcome. In public synthetic data from the care-management algorithm studied by Obermeyer et al., it found no detectable overall error but under-prediction of illness in the sickest pockets and over-prediction elsewhere, and distinguished Black patients' concentration where the score under-predicted for everyone from a Black-White gap within pockets. For APACHE IVa, an exploratory analysis located its previously reported over-prediction of ICU mortality in a mostly ventilated pocket and a low-risk pocket, with none detected in a moderate-risk one. Two chest X-ray classifiers showed offsetting local errors behind no detectable overall error. Code: https://github.com/modenesi/pocket-audit

##### **Measuring AI Agents as Teammates with Collaborative Intelligence**
2611.02633v1 by Sean Wu, Aneesh Pappu, Jiaru Zou, Yubin Kim, Yuncong Mao, Hongjian Zhou, Junchi Yu, Jieneng Chen, James Zou

Progress in AI is often measured by what a single model can do on its own. Yet teams of AI agents are starting to make breakthroughs and discoveries in science, medicine, mathematics and software engineering. As AI begins to work in teams of agents and people, the ability to collaborate becomes a core part of their intelligence. Research on human teams has measured this ability for decades. Inspired by this, we introduce the Collaborative Intelligence Benchmark, which measures how well an AI agent works as a teammate. It contains 240 tasks across 8 dimensions of teamwork, from refusing an unsafe shortcut to checking the work that a weaker model leaves behind. Each task comes from a real record or an existing benchmark, from aviation accident reports and clinical cases to coding tasks and negotiations, and has a programmatically verifiable outcome. To isolate teamwork from individual capability, models are scored only on tasks that they can solve by themselves when they have full information. We evaluate 18 closed- and open-weight models and find that every model, across sizes and families, has clear weaknesses as a teammate, with the best model scoring only 82.2 out of 100. Frontier models almost always refuse unsafe shortcuts and report misconduct, yet they cave under interpersonal pressure, reaching the best overall deal in only 14% of conflicts, and their pass rate drops from 97% to 81% when they take over a task in which a weaker model has cheated. Crucially, general capability does not guarantee good teamwork, as models with nearly the same capability score differ by up to 11 points as teammates. The benchmark includes 160 public tasks with code and data, along with 80 held-out tasks for evaluation. As AI agents increasingly work alongside other agents and people, the ability to collaborate effectively is becoming a crucial capability to measure and develop.

#### Abstracts
##### **HiVe-Mem: Interleaving Horizontal Memory Expansion and Vertical Evidence Composition for Multimodal Agents**
2611.12872v1 by Haozhen Zhang, Bohan Liu, Jianzhu Bao, Qingyuan Liu, Haodong Yue, Xiao Li, Zi Pong Lim, Wenya Wang

As multimodal agents accumulate increasingly long interaction histories, effectively retrieving and utilizing past information becomes critical for long-term interaction. Existing memory systems typically rely on semantic retrieval over compressed memories and expose predefined representations, which may miss relevant memories and overlook heterogeneous evidence needs across queries. We introduce \textbf{\hivemem}, a multimodal memory framework that interleaves \textbf{\textsc{H}}or\textbf{\textsc{i}}zontal Memory Expansion with \textbf{\textsc{Ve}}rtical Evidence Composition. For horizontal memory expansion, \hivemem organizes memory episodes into a relational graph using textual and visual anchors, expanding semantically retrieved seeds with related neighbors to recover complementary information. For vertical evidence composition, each retrieved memory retains multiple evidence forms, including compact summaries, captions, grounded visual views, raw images, and interaction history. A lightweight trainable selector independently composes an evidence subset for each retrieved memory, enabling query-adaptive utilization of heterogeneous multimodal information. This two-dimensional design jointly broadens memory coverage and adapts the evidence contributed by individual memories. Experiments across three multimodal memory benchmarks show that \hivemem consistently improves task performance across base models while reducing token consumption by about 90\% compared with the most efficient competing multimodal baselines.

摘要：

##### **LogCDM: Interpretable Log Anomaly Detection via Behavioral Graph Drift and Adaptive Signal Fusion**
2611.12775v1 by Suryansh Talukdar, Taha Sarwat, Jin Soung Yoo

Log-based anomaly detection is increasingly dominated by large pretrained models, from BERT-scale encoders to 8B-parameter language models, which require GPUs at inference time and are difficult to deploy on the monitoring nodes where logs are produced. We present LogCDM, a compact detector that scores each log session using three complementary signals: the next-event surprise of a small causal Transformer, the distance between a graph-attention embedding of the session's own transition graph and that of a normal reference graph, and a transition-drift statistic that we show is exactly a frequency-weighted chi-squared divergence between transition kernels. The divergence view yields an asymptotic null distribution, a length-standardized score, and a per-edge decomposition that explains each alarm. A sigmoid gate fuses the three signals; under a shared-covariance Gaussian model, this gate is the Bayes posterior, and its learned weights indicate which signal each dataset relies on. With 8.1-8.8M parameters and no GPU required at inference, LogCDM reaches F1 scores of 0.984 on HDFS, 0.870 on chronologically split BGL, and 0.850 on Thunderbird, within 0.013-0.12 of an 8.1B-parameter LLM detector evaluated under the same protocol and above all compact baselines on BGL. It scores a session in 5.5-10.5 ms on a CPU. Ablations show that the structural signals add 0.025-0.030 F1 on BGL and Thunderbird but almost nothing on HDFS, consistent with the learned fusion weights.

摘要：

##### **DeepChronos: Temporal Neuro-Symbolic Reasoning for Multi-Step Attack Detection**
2611.12760v1 by Sofia Burkow, Andrea Agiollo, Enkeleda Bardhi, Fernando Kuipers, Kaitai Liang, Georgios Smaragdakis

Modern cyber threats commonly unfold as multi-step attacks comprising temporally and causally related phases. Current defenses commonly follow a detect first and correlate later paradigm. Intrusion detection systems classify events independently, while downstream systems reconstruct attack progression from the resulting alerts or audit records. In this context, learning-based detectors learn attack-step dependencies implicitly as statistical regularities in the data and cannot guarantee that their predictions follow the real attack progression. Conversely, correlation systems represent attack relationships explicitly, but apply this knowledge only after the underlying attack observations have been detected. We present DEEPCHRONOS, a neuro-symbolic network intrusion detection system that shifts the paradigm from detect first and correlate later to detection through logical progression. DEEPCHRONOS combines learned flow-level evidence with explicit attack-phase rules, temporal prerequisites, and previously inferred phases, allowing the evolving attack history to inform and constrain each detection decision. Evaluations on DARPA 2000 and AIT-LDS V2 show that DEEPCHRONOS improves macro F1 over purely neural baselines by up to 11.3%, eliminates causal violations under strict temporal constraints, and maintains millisecond-scale inference latency. These results demonstrate that integrating explicit attack progression knowledge into detection produces more accurate and temporally coherent multi-step attack predictions.

摘要：

##### **GeoScope: Training-Free Program Execution beyond the Counting Horizon for Ultra-High-Resolution Remote Sensing Question Answering**
2611.12699v1 by Guohui Deng, Yuhang Jiang, Chengjun Wang, Zhongwen Hu, Zhaocong Wu, Linsheng Huang, Jinling Zhao, Chao Ruan, Miaozhong Xu, Chi Chen

In ultra-high-resolution (UHR) remote sensing images, the objects that questions count in a region or relate by direction often span only a few pixels. Existing methods mainly decide where a vision-language model (VLM) should look and trust its answer in the chosen view. We show that this assumption fails for counting, and measure VLMs as one calibrates a sensor. All eight open VLMs tested fall increasingly short once a view holds more than a certain number of objects, about five for most on DOTA aerial images. This counting horizon narrows as views grow and objects appear smaller. An empirical power-law response model predicts the undercount a split recovers and the spurious counts it adds, and supports the rule of splitting only views beyond the horizon. In GeoScope, a text-only language model parses the question into a typed spatial program that fixes the region and its resolution. The VLM only grounds and counts within its operating range, and directions are computed explicitly from the grounded boxes. To our knowledge, it is the first UHR method to split views by a measured limit of the VLM. Without training, it improves Qwen3-VL-8B by 16.3 points on regional counting and 10.5 on spatial relations in XLRS-Bench, and has the highest reported accuracy, trained methods included, on regional counting and on the whole benchmark by question. On UHR-Micro it improves global and regional counting by 16.8 and 36.2 points, 15.6 of the former from splitting. On MME-RealWorld counting it gains 11.4 points, and we show that an option that is almost never correct yet often chosen distorts comparisons there. Project page: https://avalon-s.github.io/GeoScope/

摘要：

##### **TrialDesignBench: Benchmarking Language Models for Phase-I Trial Design Against Clinical Knowledge**
2611.12640v1 by Yihan Hank Tang, Peng Yang, Jiayi Xin, Tianqi Shang, Xuyang Chen, Ying Yuan, Yi Lian, Qi Long

AI is used in much of the clinical trial pipeline. However, selecting the statistical design of a trial, a crucial step that determines how drug doses are tested on humans and evaluated, has seen almost no AI progress. LLMs appear to be a natural fit given their strengths in biomedical knowledge and statistical reasoning. Nevertheless, there is currently no appropriate way to evaluate such abilities because recent regulatory and methodological shifts, most notably FDA's Project Optimus, have redefined what constitutes a reasonable design and rendered many previous designs obsolete. To lay the groundwork for AI-assisted trial design, we introduce TrialDesignBench, a benchmark of 617 Phase-I/I-II oncology dose-finding trials, each paired with an expert-validated reference design that is consistent with the latest FDA regulatory initiatives. This reference standard is produced through a formalization of the expert design process. We evaluate five frontier LLMs, none of which matches the reference standard. Our ablation experiments trace this performance gap to two main causes, inference of latent, unrecorded design determinants from trial sources (supplying them raises class-wise balanced accuracy by 0.22) and structured decision-tree reasoning (+0.24). TrialDesignBench offers a reproducible, contemporary-standard objective for evaluating and enhancing AI-assisted trial design.

摘要：

##### **Beyond Sunny Skies: Using Large Language Models to Predict Urban Traffic in Adverse Weather**
2611.12635v1 by Zhu Xiao, Kuan Feng, Tong Li, Chenxi Liu, Gang Wang, Zhuo Tang, Kenli Li

Urban traffic flow prediction is a cornerstone of intelligent transportation systems, yet accurate forecasting under adverse weather conditions remains a significant challenge. Existing data-driven models, while effective for routine patterns, often struggle with the sparse data and complex non-linear shifts associated with rare meteorological events. These methods typically treat environmental contexts as shallow numerical features, failing to capture the underlying semantic reasoning-why and how specific adverse conditions alter mobility behaviors across regions with different functional compositions. To address this limitation, we propose Multi-STG, a unified framework that integrates Large Language Model (LLM)-derived semantic priors with graph-based spatiotemporal learning. Multi-STG introduces an LLM-empowered semantic enhancement module that transforms comprehensive context descriptions such as weather, POIs, and holidays into high-level semantic embeddings, effectively providing a knowledge-driven complement to data scarcity. Furthermore, we design a multimodal attentional fusion mechanism to dynamically weigh heterogeneous inputs and a semantic-enhanced prediction head with gated residual calibration to robustly adjust forecasts based on semantic guidance. Extensive experiments on real-world multi-city private car datasets demonstrate that Multi-STG outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, reducing MAE by 11.82\% on average relative to the strongest baseline across three cities, while maintaining stable performance under sparse and non-stationary conditions.

摘要：

##### **Type-logical grammars and formal languages**
2611.12618v1 by Richard Moot

We show that type-logical grammars with the standard rules for product, associativity, extraction and infixation have an NP-complete fixed recognition problem, adapting a construction for first-order linear logic from Pshenitsyn (2025), who already showed that grammars in first-order linear logic are closed under intersection and therefore not semilinear and not mildly context-sensitive. We strengthen these results and shown that grammars in first-order linear logic can simulate the quasi-linear grammars of Book \& Greibach (1970), the grammars recognised in non-deterministic linear time (NSPACE(n) or. NLIN). I argue that NLIN is an interesting class of formal languages as well, and a useful upper bound on the time complexity of formalisms used for natural language, just like LOGCFL is a useful upper bound on the space complexity. Taken together this means that first-order linear logic can be seen as the logic of non-deterministic linear-time string languages, graph languages and automata.

摘要：

##### **AuDiR: Diagnosing Audio Reasoning with Rule and Percept Interventions**
2611.12478v1 by Chengwei Wei, Yi Ma, Nancy F. Chen

Answer accuracy alone does not distinguish failures in acoustic perception, rule knowledge, and rule application. AuDiR (Audio Diagnostic Reasoning) examines these distinctions through controlled input interventions. Each item contains an audio clip, a multiple-choice question, a task-general rule, and a textual description of the acoustic observations needed to answer. Three conditions progressively supply the rule and observations; a fourth removes the audio while retaining the question and rule. The first correct diagnostic condition assigns an operational response label, without identifying a unique internal cause. The current benchmark contains 201 items in 18 families drawn from 9 sources, with answer references documented separately from percept extraction. In a preliminary evaluation of Gemini-3.8-flash, accuracy is 29.3% without supplied information, 31.3% with the rule, and 98.5% with the observations also supplied. No-audio accuracy is 23.9%, with a confidence interval containing the 25.0% chance rate. A family-level knowledge probe associates incomplete rule recall with larger gains from rule provision. These results show the benefit of supplying acoustic observations as text, while incomplete validation, possible recognition shortcuts, and non-monotonic responses limit stronger failure-attribution claims.

摘要：

##### **Pricing Symbolic Rules by What GNNs Leave Unlearned**
2611.12446v1 by Satabarto Sarkar, Prithwineel Paul

Symbolic knowledge (motif counts, ring counts, label constraints) is routinely added to graph neural networks (GNNs) and justified by their limited expressivity. We replace this yardstick with an exact one. The largest log-loss reduction a rule $S$ can give a model family is $I(Y;S\midσ)$ given what the family computes; every realised gain splits exactly into this term plus repaid neural debt; and for a trained network with representation $T$ the value is $I(Y;S\mid T)$, the label information training failed to extract. Rule design thus becomes rule discovery: we score candidates on the network's out-of-fold residual against calibrated permutation nulls with family-wise error control. On ZINC, discovery recovers the hidden ring term $\max(0,\ell_{\max}-6)$ of the target in three independent runs ($p_{\mathrm{adj}}=0.001$) and beats expert cycle counts; frozen and applied to five unseen datasets it is never significantly worse than the plain network or the expert rule, and on BBBP it significantly beats both.

摘要：

##### **AutoBuddy: Evaluating Proactive, Always-On Agents on Task Discovery and Execution in a Simulated Workplace**
2611.12393v1 by Shiqi Chen, Daniel Madrigal Diaz, Wei-Ning Chen, Gbola Afonja, Fangkai Yang, Rujia Wang, Chetan Bansal, Victor Rühle, Saravan Rajmohan, Dongge Han

As LLM agents become more autonomous, they are increasingly expected to run as always-on assistants on a user's behalf, identifying work to be done and completing it without being asked. Yet existing benchmarks usually give the agent its task, whereas in knowledge work the requests sit in the user's workspace and the agent must first recognise which obligations exist. We study proactive task discovery and execution, in which an agent must infer a user's pending tasks from their workspace and complete them in the systems where the work lives. This setting poses two challenges. For the agent, the tasks are buried in a large volume of mail, chat, documents and records. For evaluation, a benchmark needs a simulated organisation whose people, events and documents are mutually consistent, so that tasks arise realistically and their correct outcomes are known. We introduce AutoBuddy-Bench, a simulator that addresses these challenges. It models an organisation's employees, projects, business systems and event history as a temporal graph, renders it into realistic workspace artefacts, derives ground-truth tasks from the events that create obligations, and computes each task's outcome from the world state; execution is judged by the agent's changes to the workspace. To evaluate current LLMs, we build AutoBuddy, a reference agent with interchangeable backbone LLMs: it identifies tasks from the workspace, grounding each in its source records, and executes each in a separate session with write access. Varying only the backbone, we find a systematic shift in where tasks are lost: weaker models fail mainly at discovery, intermediate models identify tasks but often stop short of carrying them out, and the strongest models fail mainly during execution, most often on business-system operations. Discovery precision is only weakly related to execution quality, suggesting the two are distinct capabilities.

摘要：

##### **Reconstructing hourly traffic on every street of a capital from a sparse counting network: a graph neural network applied to Paris**
2611.12379v1 by Xavier Jurado, C{é}dric Wemmert, Nicolas Reiminger

Micro-scale air quality models require hourly traffic data on every road segment, whereas permanent counters cover only a small part of the network. A graph neural network (GNN) was developed to reconstruct hourly traffic flows on the 32,163 road segments of Paris intra-muros from the 1,573 segments equipped with a counter (4.9%). The model adapts the Graph Neural Network for Urban Interpolation (GNNUI) to a real, sparse counting network. Flows are decoded on the edges of the junction graph (one edge per segment, both directions summed), a traffic climatology serves as an offset in a zero-inflated negative-binomial likelihood, and training proceeds by masking counters. Eight-fold spatial cross-validation, with counters sharing a published series merged before splitting, distinguishes interpolation at a withheld counter in an instrumented district (R${}^2$ = 0.89, error of a quarter of the mean flow) from extrapolation to a district without counters (R${}^2$ = 0.74, a third of the mean flow). These scores hold on main axes where counters are located, while relative errors are about 50% on the few counted local streets. Ablations show that the climatology offset tends to help extrapolation. Road attributes, time cycles and the live state of the counting network matter most, while weather inputs have no significant effect. The masking rate is the only inherited hyperparameter with a clear effect. When evaluated on January--August 2026 (outside the training period), the frozen model stayed around the error band of previous years.

摘要：

##### **TANSAR: Editable and Attributable Correlation Matrix Memories under Correlated Keys, with Rule-Constrained Multi-Hop Knowledge Editing**
2611.12374v1 by Abdulhady Abas Abdulla, Jiyan Salim Mahmud, Fatemeh Daneshfar, Hussein Mohammed Ali, Mourad Oussalah, Muhammad Bilal

Correlation Matrix Memories (CMMs) store associations as sums of rank-one outer products, providing closed-form addressability, exact deletion, and linear attribution. While classical analyses assume random, orthogonal keys, semantic encoders produce correlated representations. We analyse CMMs under correlated keys using frame-theoretic coherence: (i) capacity bounds yielding an encoder-independent limit $n \le d(1+\varepsilon^2)$ for mean squared crosstalk $\varepsilon^2$; (ii) an exact characterisation of the collateral effect of deleting an association, guaranteeing that deletion never invalidates certified associations; and (iii) a geometric transparency bound demonstrating why attribution requires candidate sets of centred keys. On this basis, we introduce Topology-Aware Neuro-Symbolic Associative Retrieval (TANSAR). TANSAR traverses a directed acyclic association graph, prunes invalid paths via sound ground Horn constraints, and injects a representation derived strictly from the verified path into the residual stream of a frozen language model, guarded by a Clopper--Pearson selective-risk gate. Controlled experiments confirm all theoretical bounds. In multi-hop knowledge editing, Horn pruning reduces answer contradictions from 25.1% to 0.5%, whereas Hopfield retrieval incurs 24% contradictions when stale associations remain stored (and 0% if stale associations are removed). An end-to-end edit transaction requires 13.6 ms on a standard CPU and 2.37 ms on GPU. The symbolic component matches gold labels across all 600 ProofWriter theories and achieves 94.0% agreement on FOLIO, demonstrating sound and efficient multi-hop associative retrieval with exact unlearning.

摘要：

##### **How Many Exposures Does a World Model Need? Learning Unseen Game Mechanics at Test Time**
2611.12324v1 by Ku Onoda, Haruto Kawano, Shota Takashiro, Kohei Hayashi

World models enable agents to plan and learn from imagined experience, but in an unfamiliar environment they must learn its mechanics from limited interaction. A mechanic is a rule for how an action or an interaction changes part of the environment. Each occurrence of a mechanic that the model observes is one exposure. We ask how many exposures a world model needs to predict the effects of a new environment's mechanics. We address this question on ARC-AGI-3, a benchmark of interactive games defined by their programs. We introduce MechEval, which labels 69 recurring mechanics across 20 games from these programs. It scores each prediction on the cells the mechanic changes at each exposure. As a test bed, we build WARC (World model for ARC-AGI-3), a 35M-parameter vision transformer that is pretrained on other games and updates all of its parameters after each transition. On held-out games, WARC learns to predict four in five mechanics with at least 80% accuracy on the cells they change and keeps that accuracy to the end of the recordings. Half of them reach that accuracy within about 80 exposures, in a single play of the game, and without updates only one in seven does at all. In closed-loop play on the public games, WARC-Agent, which pairs WARC with a fixed graph explorer, triples the explorer's official efficiency score from 0.15 to 0.46, mostly through test-time training. On the hidden games of the Kaggle leaderboard, our entry scores 0.20.

摘要：

##### **MNN-Pocket-0.3B Technical Report**
2611.12311v1 by  MNN Team

This technical report presents MNN-Pocket-0.3B, a compact 300M-parameter dense language model designed for efficient on-device deployment and supporting context lengths of up to 32K tokens. With an INT4 deployment footprint of only 173 MB and decoding throughput of up to 466 tokens/s on iPad Pro M5 (Metal), it can be seamlessly integrated into applications on edge devices such as smartphones and smart glasses. MNN-Pocket-0.3B is developed through the co-design of its architecture, data, training pipeline, and inference system. Hardware-aware architecture selection and dependency-aware vocabulary pruning balance model capability with on-device memory and compute constraints. Extensive evaluations demonstrate that MNN-Pocket-0.3B achieves frontier-level performance among models of comparable size, with particularly strong results in Chinese and English knowledge and reasoning, mathematics, coding, and instruction following. It consistently outperforms similarly sized open-source models and even surpasses several 0.5B-parameter models on instruction-following benchmarks. We release MNN-Pocket-0.3B with open weights and ready-to-use MNN deployment packages. Two laws follow. We build a construction that reaches this far, checked in Lean, and, when vertices carry no names, no such network reaches further. ##### **The Value of Not Knowing: How Fresh Eyes through Naive LLMs Fuel Creativity**
2611.11725v1 by David B. D'Ambrosio, Alon Albalak, Joel Lehman, Kenneth O. Stanley

Many major scientific advances come from approaching a problem with a new or novel perspective. While large language models (LLMs) with internet-scale knowledge seem poised to take such perspectives, their consistent generation remains elusive. Instead, models are drawn to dominant views and preexisting, in-field bias. To explore the potential of fresh eyes to increase creativity, this paper proposes naive LLMs, models that are made intentionally ignorant of a specific topic. Through deprivation in training or intentional forgetting, naive models are unburdened by existing dogma, allowing them to be free to make new associations and conjectures. These naive models can then be paired with a stronger mentor model that can refine their unique perspectives into concrete proposals. For the 1B and 7B models tested in this paper, naive models consistently produce seed ideas that are more novel and useful than baseline counterparts, and mentor refinement improves the ideas even further. Thus, contrary to the prevailing tradition of scaling up training data, we demonstrate a case where less information is actually more powerful, in particular when the aim is to discover genuinely outside-the-box solutions.

摘要：

##### **Optimizing Multi-Tier Intermediation in Programmatic Advertising: A DAG-Based Mechanism for Supply Path Optimization**
2611.11651v1 by Amelia Bădică, Costin Bădică

Digital programmatic advertising operates through complex, multi-hop supply chains. The opacity of these intermediary networks leads to significant efficiency losses, often referred to as the ``ad-tech tax.'' While current industry practices utilize heuristic Supply Path Optimization (SPO) to trim inefficient routes, they fail to optimize the equilibrium of the entire network. This paper extends previous work on Directed Acyclic Graph (DAG) intermediation models by applying them to traceable programmatic supply chains mapped via the OpenRTB SupplyChain object. We introduce multi-attribute utility functions and Mixed-Integer Linear Programming (MILP) to account for substitutable routing (OR nodes) and percentage-based fee structures. Furthermore, we outline a computational methodology and experimental framework using public \texttt{schain} and \texttt{sellers.json} data to benchmark optimal Nash social welfare against current uncoordinated market outcomes.

摘要：

##### **Perturbing Inputs Improves Pretraining Distillation**
2611.11598v1 by Ziyang Cai, Yihe Dong, Dimitris Papailiopoulos, Shivam Garg

Knowledge distillation trains a student language model to match a teacher's predictions on the training text. We ask whether learning the teacher's behavior beyond the training text can further improve the student. We investigate this using three input perturbations: additive token noise, blending two documents, and blending neighboring tokens. Specifically, for an early part of pretraining we perturb the inputs and train the student to match the teacher on them, and we spend the rest of the same token budget on ordinary distillation. When pretraining from scratch, the best method improves on standard distillation at both scales we tried, by 0.025 nats at 135M parameters and by 0.020 nats at 0.5B (mean of three seeds). At 135M this is 1.45 times the gain that distillation itself provides over next-token training. We also observe promising downstream transfer: at 0.5B, the best variant improves average zero-shot accuracy by about one percentage point across seven tasks. It also retains its validation-loss advantage after low-bit weight quantization. We also test a data-limited setting in which training data must be reused, and find that the gains persist. Finally, ablations suggest that, for additive noise, perturbing the teacher's inputs contributes most of the gain, while perturbing only the student's inputs yields much less.

##### **FinCutBench: A Continually Updated Benchmark for Measuring the Temporal Boundaries of LLM Financial Knowledge**
2611.11588v1 by Siyuan Li, Ke Wan, Boyong Hou, Yujie Deng, Zixuan Yuan

In financial backtesting and other point-in-time tasks, external inputs can be restricted by date, but model parameters may retain financial facts disclosed later, introducing parametric look-ahead bias. We introduce FinCutBench, a continually updated monthly financial knowledge benchmark for measuring how far a fixed model's financial knowledge extends over calendar time. The current dataset contains 9,000 Chinese and English four-option multiple-choice questions from January 2023 to September 2026, covering Chinese and U.S. markets and four categories of financial information. We construct monthly knowledge profiles using the approximate months in which the tested facts became public. After a profile passes the decline test and knot checks, we define the FinCut Boundary as the first month when the fitted profile reaches its lower level, and report the stability of the estimated date under resampling. Boundary dates and localization precision vary across models. Using the independently measured Transition Start and Boundary as fixed references for downstream sample grouping, we find clear relative declines after the Boundary in U.S. asset ranking and earnings recall for some models, while Chinese-market ranking estimates vary across models and all eight disclosure-period-paired forecast-error comparisons have unadjusted $p$ values above 0.10. The FinCut Boundary thus provides an empirical reference for the temporal scope of parametric knowledge in point-in-time financial evaluation, supporting evaluation-window selection and result interpretation.

##### **Weight-Space Programming of Variational Quantum Circuits: Large Language Models Write Quantum Circuit Parameters Without an Optimizer**
2611.11537v1 by Debarshi Kundu, Swaroop Ghosh, Vasant G Honavar

Variational quantum algorithms (VQAs) optimize circuit angles through repeated quantum-device evaluations, often facing vanishing gradients and high optimization costs. We ask whether a large language model (LLM) can write these angles directly. Extending *weight-space programming* from classical networks to quantum circuits, we provide a frontier LLM (\gpt) with the problem, circuit, and gate conventions. Its angles are executed exactly as written, without returning losses, gradients, or accuracy feedback. Across 139 requests, we test handwritten-digit classification, VQE ground-state estimation for spin chains and H$_2$, and MaxCut QAOA, comparing against random weights, classical optimization, and optimization initialized with LLM weights on the same circuit. When parameters have interpretable structure, the LLM matches or approaches the optimizer: it achieves 95--100\% of the energy spectrum on every VQE problem, derives the exact H$_2$ ground state in closed form, finds optimal depth-one QAOA angles for every graph, and reaches 93--96\% accuracy on digits 3 versus 8 with 12--18 hand-written weights, outperforming an optimizer trained on the same ten examples. Its weights are also strong warm starts: optimization reaches its best value after a median of one evaluation rather than hundreds. Allowed to design circuits, the LLM chooses data-dependent rotation chains encoding pixel importance in all 30 cases (one qubit for binary tasks, one chain per qubit for ten classes), reaching 65\% accuracy across ten digits. Yet the approach fails in opaque weight spaces: accuracy drops to chance when fixed circuits exceed 30 weights, while optimization improves. It is also sensitive to gate conventions: 6 of 42 QAOA replies reverse mixing-angle signs and fail. We release all prompts, replies, and scores.

##### **DriveFoundry: Co-Evolving Data and Model for Efficient Closed-Loop Iteration in Autonomous Driving**
2611.11532v1 by Ziyang Yao, Yuncheng Jiang, Fang Li, Haochen Liu, Shaoqing Xu, Wen Zhang, Xiangyang Chu, Zibin Guo, Jiale Hou, Chuang Wan, Jia Hua, Gengzhao Xiang, Xiaoran Luo, Chong He, Yuechen Luo, Junzhe Wang, Zhiyu Sun, Jun Zhang, Wei Li, Lei Li, Hongwei Xie, Bing Wang, Yuannan Shen, Jianwei Cui, Guang Chen, Hangjun Ye, Jingwei Zhao

A driving foundation model must learn reusable decision knowledge from rare critical events and apply it under strict runtime constraints. We introduce DriveFoundry, a closed-loop framework improving iteration efficiency under data and computation budgets by coupling structured causal data refinement with adaptive inference. The Data Engine builds traceable scene-candidate records linking scene evidence and factor-decision relations to candidate trajectories and outcome assessments. Candidate comparisons and counterfactual annotations clarify how scene factors affect behavioral feasibility and preference. Verified model failures guide VLM-assisted annotation and expert correction, refining training data and annotation systems. The model engine, Xiaomi-Drive-o1, unifies Perception, Causation, Action, World, and Value within a shared vision-language backbone to enable cross-task knowledge transfer. Block-wise latent refill passes candidate-specific evidence and predicted visual consequences between task blocks, connecting scene understanding, planning, and evaluation. A learned value-of-computation head selects tasks and allocates latent tokens based on expected benefit and cost, adapting inference to each decision. On PhysicalAI, the 9B model achieves the lowest ADE@1 across evaluated splits and horizons among tested models. In AlpaSim, it outperforms Alpamayo 1.5 across all metrics and achieves lower close-encounter and off-road rates than the 34B Alpamayo 2 Super, while trailing in progress and AlpaSim scores. For the 0.8B variant, joint training reduces 5 s minADE@6 by about 54% without altering the inference path or token budget. Adaptive inference matches full reasoning performance using only 28% of tokens. Real-vehicle tests confirm runtime feasibility, supporting a unified paradigm for learning from critical experience and dynamically allocating computation.

##### **Hydra: Towards Transferable Multi-Task Learning on Temporal Graphs**
2611.11520v1 by Kiarash Shamsi, Farimah Poursafaei, Tran Gia Bao Ngo, Reihaneh Rabbany, Baris Coskunuzer, Guillaume Rabusseau, Michael M. Bronstein, Shenyang Huang, Cuneyt Gurcan Akcora

Real-world evolving networks are naturally modeled as temporal graphs (TGs), where capturing temporal dynamics is essential for predicting future graph properties that support downstream decision-making. Existing temporal graph methods have been developed primarily for single-task prediction, and little is known about their generalization across tasks or transfer to unseen networks. This leaves the challenge of multi-task graph property prediction in TGs largely open. We address this challenge by introducing Hydra, a novel architecture that integrates local connectivity features from temporal GNNs with a spectral learning module that captures global connectivity patterns. This design enables joint learning of local and global information under a multi-task objective. In multi-task classification, Hydra achieves an 8.9% relative gain in AUC over the strongest competitor. In multi-task regression, Hydra achieves competitive results in all three tasks, while obtaining the best results in two tasks with a 8.2% relative gain in MAE compared to the strongest baseline. Moreover, Hydra delivers these gains with a 22% reduction in training time compared to temporal transfer models. These results provide the first systematic evidence that multi-task transferable learning on temporal graphs is effective. By delivering consistent top-ranked performance, Hydra highlights multi-task training on temporal graphs as a promising direction toward adaptable foundation models for temporal graphs.

##### **JoMoHa: Joint Model--Harness Optimization with Reinforcement Learning**
2611.11499v1 by Xudong Wu, Guangchen Lan, Mingqi Yuan, Jiayu Chen

The performance of language agents depends jointly on model weights and the harness. Optimizing either alone can be constrained by the other component, limiting overall performance gains. Our analysis of naive alternating training reveals progressive incompatibility between the model and harness, which increasingly interferes with task execution and may also undermine model training, leading to performance degradation. To coordinate the evolution of models and harnesses, we introduce JoMoHa, to our knowledge the first framework to jointly train an executable harness generation policy and a task execution policy through reinforcement learning. JoMoHa outperforms the evaluated weight-only and harness-only baselines and achieves the highest success rates among the evaluated methods on ALFWorld, WebShop, and DBBench, averaging 70.3% and reaching 97.0% on ALFWorld. By learning both task execution and runtime improvement from its own generated trajectories, JoMoHa performs recursive self-improvement (RSI).

##### **Robust and Private Swarm Learning via Zero-Knowledge Validator Admission and Consensus Aggregation**
2611.11477v1 by Marco Arazzi, Mert Cihangiroglu, Antonino Nocera

Swarm Learning improves resilience and removes the need for a single trusted coordinator by distributing model aggregation and validation across participating nodes. However, it also delegates the validation of model updates to peers whose private data may be biased, unrepresentative, or adversarial, and existing Swarm Learning protocols generally do not attest that data, leaving decentralized validation as an attack surface. We present a robust, privacy-preserving Swarm Learning framework that makes verifiable data quality the condition for admission to the learning task. A validator joins only after a zero-knowledge attestation proves, without revealing any raw sample, that a reference model ran correctly on its private data. The attested outputs also yield a label-free class profile, so the network assembles a collectively IID validator pool from individually skewed validators as accurately as an oracle with the true labels (total-variation distance to uniform 0.025, against 0.021 for the oracle and 0.21 for random selection). Admitted validators score client updates and, through a Tendermint-style Byzantine Fault Tolerant consensus, agree on a gradient mask and a quality-derived aggregation weight for each submission, replacing dataset-size weighting with a quality-aware signal. Adapting the cut-layer idea of Split Learning, a challenge-based audit lets peers re-verify that a mask was computed on the attested data without an expensive end-to-end zero-knowledge proof. Across four benchmarks under non-IID partitions the framework matches FedAvg on clean data; under a gradient-scaling attack that collapses FedAvg to random chance, it retains 94% of clean accuracy on CIFAR-100 and over 99% on FashionMNIST, where FedAvg achieves only 2.1% and 11%. The result is a private, robust, and auditable foundation for decentralized learning.

##### **GOPR-CAD: Graph-of-Operations Progressive Reasoning for CAD Generation**
2611.11470v1 by Yitian Pang, Yifan Li, Xiaowei Yue, Li Zheng

Generating accurate CAD models requires substantial engineering expertise and careful control of geometry, topology, and mechanical constraints. Text-to-CAD aims to simplify this process, but current LLM-based methods often generate CAD programs directly. Language models remain unreliable when producing long code while coordinating coupled geometric, topological, and dimensional constraints. Consequently, executable programs may still produce structurally incorrect models, which execution rate and rendered appearance may fail to reveal. To address these limitations, we propose GOPR-CAD, an agentic Text-to-CAD pipeline based on Graph-of-Operations Progressive Reasoning. A language model acts as a decision agent over encapsulated CAD operations and progressively constructs an executable operation graph, which a deterministic compiler converts into the final CAD model. This separation requires no additional training and supports different language models. To evaluate structural fidelity, we construct MHCAD, a benchmark of 300 standard mechanical part requirements derived from Machinery's Handbook. Their explicit structures enable reference-free STEP/B-rep evaluation. GOPR-CAD achieves 82.7% Structural Yield on MHCAD, and 87.2% Structural Yield on an external CADPrompt subset.

##### **How to "train" a surgeon? Benchmarking generalist agents in surgical scene understanding**
2611.11437v1 by Gengyuan Zhang, Xiao Han, Shijie Zhou, Abdullah Koukash, Jingpei Wu, Jindong Gu, Yuqicheng Zhu, Nassir Navab, Volker Tresp, Ghazal Ghazaei

Evaluating general-purpose multimodal large language models (MLLMs) for surgical scene understanding remains limited due to scarce surgical data and fragmented evaluation protocols. We address these challenges through a two-stage evaluation framework for surgical scene understanding. In Stage I, we introduce SurgKnowBench, a diagnostic benchmark that harmonizes public surgical datasets across procedures and tasks and evaluates proprietary and open-source MLLMs to map the boundary of pretrained surgical competence. SurgKnowBench reveals that current models retain partial visual priors but remain weak on relational and procedural understanding and workflow assessment. Building on this failure frontier and motivated by the scaffolded nature of surgical training, we introduce SurgAgentGym, an agentic environment where general-purpose MLLM agents can use bounded knowledge search over SurgWiki and general-purpose visual grounding for surgical tasks. Our results show that agentic inference narrows the gap mainly for perception-heavy tasks, while fine-grained localization and expert-style assessment remain persistent bottlenecks. ##### **Vietnamese Legal Ontology: Every legal answer, traced to the article that grounds it**
2611.11426v1 by Khac-Hoai Nam Bui, Quan Tran Minh

A legal answer is only as good as the article it rests on. Vietnam's largest open legal corpus pairs 109,543 published questions and answers with 573,851 legal documents, yet its citations are free text: "Khoan 3 Dieu 101 cua Bo luat nay" ("Clause 3, Article 101 of this Code") names neither the code nor its version. Following the five stages of NVIDIA Auto Ontology, Discover, Ground, Validate, Expose and Learn, we turn four open datasets into a knowledge graph of 11.5 M nodes and 16.0 M edges in which every node and edge carries its source record and method, with no duplicate keys, dangling edges or schema violations. Of 67,868 cited provisions, 61,487 resolve to a dated law version, 51,694 to a parsed article, clause or point, and 19,012 are confirmed word for word. The ontology then shows what flat records hide. 36% of the 78,361 answers whose citations resolve now cite a law that is no longer in force; 2,525 verified citations point to an article number that says something else in the current law; 2,520 citations were re-versioned because their words match a different version than their date suggests; and 5,575 document numbers are shared by several local authorities, one of them by 72. Every number in this paper is computed from the graph.

##### **AOP-Smart-Finder: A High-Throughput Human-AI Hybrid Framework for AOP Discovery and Evidence Mapping from Large-Scale Literature**
2611.11349v1 by Qinjiang Niu, Lu Yan

The rapid growth of scientific literature and the high heterogeneity of biological terminology and mechanistic descriptions make it difficult for researchers to integrate scattered research evidence, identify connections among biological events, and construct mechanistic networks. Here, we developed a high-throughput human-AI hybrid framework, AOP-Smart-Finder, for biological event discovery, relationship mining, and Adverse Outcome Pathway (AOP) construction and evidence mapping from large-scale literature. This framework integrates Large Language Models (LLMs), AOP-Smart retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) technology, and Python-based automation, supporting two analysis modes: one based on a single compound and the other based on a predefined set of biological events. In the di(2-ethylhexyl) phthalate (DEHP) case, the system processed 12,983 articles within 30 min and included 1,093 of them, from which it extracted 1,589 biological events (including 458 official key events [KEs] and 1,131 candidate KEs) and 2,519 key event relationships (KERs). In the amyotrophic lateral sclerosis (ALS) case, the system identified 55 stressors from 437 articles and extracted 278 biological events and 548 event relationships. Following manual curation and Weight of Evidence (WoE) evaluation, a mechanistic network comprising 16 KEs and 23 KERs was ultimately constructed. This framework can either use existing AOPs as knowledge guidance for mechanistic expansion and evidence supplementation, or discover novel event connections and construct mechanistic networks around user-specified research topics. It provides a high-throughput, traceable, human-AI hybrid methodology for complex biological mechanism elucidation and AOP discovery driven by large-scale literature.

##### **Separation of Skilling Context for Standard Operating Procedure-based Agents**
2611.11348v1 by Mitsuhiro Nakayama, Toshiaki Sota, Takehiko Yamaguchi, Michiaki Tatsubori

Standard Operating Procedures (SOPs) are kept concise for human readability, so LLM agents that generate executable skills from them lack the tacit knowledge and implementation details needed for automation. Human intervention fills these gaps, but must be repeated as SOPs evolve---a problem we call \textit{SOP-skill drift}. We propose managing SOPs and Contextual Knowledge (CK) separately, together with a \textit{contract-aware} evaluation that distinguishes genuine violations from CK-resolved differences. On eight Indeed-derived workflows (single LLM), it raises the functional-equivalence rate from 12\% to 58\%. Under three SOP-revision perturbations, previously extracted CK already covers 79.5\% of newly introduced automation gaps.
