##### **Agentic Attribution of Cloud Resource Ownership**
2611.11340v1 by Tiltan Gilat, Gal Engelberg, Ariel Zadok, Leon Goldberg

Security tools raise findings across cloud environments, and AI agents now investigate and prioritize them. A finding is still resolved by people: it has to reach the people responsible for fixing it and accountable for the resource it concerns. Ownership is the organizational context that decides who that is. Where recorded, it is declared by hand in configuration management databases or owner tags and goes stale unless kept current; accounts are shared, and automation creates much of the infrastructure. The knowledge sits in work records, code history, audit logs and identity directories, encoded in each organization's own conventions. Recovering it poses four challenges: the signal is scattered across sources, none of them authoritative; the conventions that carry it differ between organizations; reaching an owner takes multi-hop links; and those related to a resource differ in the strength of their relation. We define ownership as a set of entities, people or teams, each related to the resource by one of five patterns ordered by strength. We present a reference architecture for agentic ownership attribution that derives ownership from these records instead of declaring it: it profiles an organization offline into tested signal procedures and an ownership playbook, then attributes each resource with a coordinator agent and parallel investigators, with confidence computed by rules. On 100 resources of 50 Amazon Web Services (AWS) resource types in one organization, we pool the architecture's candidates with those of an unprimed general-purpose agent and verify every evidence piece against the records. The architecture reaches 92.1% weighted recall and 86.6% weighted precision, against 54.8% and 77.4%, at 0.55 times the agent's cost per unit of coverage. The difference lies in people who build and change resources without an explicit owner, found through work records and code history.

##### **ReGKG-2025: A GKG-Compatible Reconstruction of the GDELT Global Knowledge Graph for the Missing Period of 14 June to 2 July 2025**
2611.11316v1 by Nikita A. Klimin

The June 2025 outage in the Global Database of Events, Language, and Tone (GDELT) collection pipeline left a gap in the Global Knowledge Graph (GKG), interrupting article-level news metadata used in downstream research. ReGKG-2025 is an archive-derived sample of 281,620 reconstructed article records covering all 1,665 missing 15-minute batches. Articles were recovered from Common Crawl and the Wayback Machine using source quotas estimated from surrounding native GKG data. Text was screened and normalized, non-English bodies were translated, and flagged records were manually reviewed. Eligible articles were converted to the 27-column GKG 2.1 layout. The versioned package includes record-level provenance, archive replay and WARC locators, source summaries, source-by-day calibration weights, and the complete manual decision journal. The sample reaches about 14% of modeled native volume and represents sources accounting for 84% of selected reference record mass. In an intact-week test, 41% of recovered articles matched native GKG records; field agreement on matched pairs is close to the processor baseline (tone MAE 1.69). ##### **Least-Privilege Auditing of Agent Tool Calls with Intent Graphs and Reasoning-Guided Escalation**
2611.11305v1 by Doron Shavit

LLM agents that call tools are exposed to indirect prompt injection: instructions hidden in tool outputs can steer an agent into calls the user never asked for. We present a runtime audit that derives an executable intent graph from the user's objective and the deployment's tool catalog with one LLM call per task, and checks every tool call against it with a deterministic loop that makes no LLM call (median 5.7 microseconds per call). Two further mechanisms recover utility. Lookups of the user's own state bypass the audit, because the harm of an attack lies in its write or outward step. The calls that remain flagged are escalated to a reasoning guardian, in the spirit of tool-integrated reasoning models such as START: a reasoning model that sees only the objective, the plan, the call history without tool outputs, and the flagged call, and can verify facts with a restricted Python-expression tool. A current agent showed no baseline attack success, so end-to-end validation remains open.

##### **ErosionBench: Probing Alignment Decay in Enterprise Multi-Agent Systems**
2611.11266v1 by Riya Bharathwaj, Akshara Prabhakar, Roshan Ram, Avni Kothari, Sarah Tan, Huan Wang, Silvio Savarese

Each scenario is authored as a deterministic state graph before any prose is rendered, so every state change traces to a known cause. It spans four enterprise domains and four decay mechanisms across 1,728 scenarios with matched benign controls, labeled against the OWASP Top-10 for LLM Applications, plus 198 image-based attacks, each paired with a text equivalent, so a defense must survive the same attack in either channel. Perimeter defenses suppress attacks only by over-refusing 54-92% of legitimate work, so a low attack-success rate can conceal severe over-refusal. Since it monitors trajectory, not surface content, its detection is channel-invariant: the strongest auditor flags image-delivered attacks about as reliably as their text equivalents (recall gap $\leq$2 points per model). ##### **MCSA: Multi-Cutoff Spectral Aggregation in SO(3) Equivariant GNNs for Interatomic Potentials**
2611.11226v1 by Junfu Tan, Mengfan Wu, Yu Zhu, Yu Shi, Yifang Qin, Chang Liu, Bonan Zhu, Ziheng Lu

Machine Learning Interatomic Potentials (MLIPs) based on Equivariant Graph Neural Networks (EGNNs) have achieved remarkable progress in atomistic modeling. However, accurately representing atomic interactions across different spatial scales remains challenging. In this work, we investigate the radial spectral properties of SO(3)-equivariant message passing and establish that the cutoff radius controls the characteristic spatial-frequency scale of radial interactions. Based on this analysis, we propose Multi-Cutoff Spectral Aggregation (MCSA), a plug-and-play module that aggregates radial kernels with different cutoff radii to enrich multi-scale geometric representations. To achieve efficient implementation, we exploit the compact support of radial kernels to fuse cutoff-dependent radial weights before angular tensor-product operations, avoiding redundant angular computations while preserving rotational equivariance. Extensive experiments across representative EGNN backbones demonstrate consistent improvements in energy, force, and stress prediction, as well as enhanced accuracy in higher-order properties, including phonon dispersion and lattice thermal conductivity. Further evaluations on layered materials with distinct interaction scales demonstrate the effectiveness of physically motivated cutoff selection. Code is available at: https://github.com/JF-Tan/MCSA.

Automated formalization makes such arguments mechanically checkable, and automated proving goes further by discovering machine-checked proofs for statements whose proofs are incomplete or missing altogether. Both require reconstructing assumptions from source materials, accessing specialized mathematical infrastructure and managing long proof developments. A natural-language proof, when available, guides its search but is not required: from a statement alone, the prover develops the argument itself and constructs a Lean proof. Its workflow integrates source preparation, statement review, library supported planning and proof construction. Dynamic proof dependency graphs and task groups support parallel, resumable proof development. Statlib's shared definitions and theorem interfaces are organized for retrieval and composition by agents, and its coverage of statistics keeps expanding. Verified results and usage feedback from the prover inform reviewed library updates, so that proof development drives this expansion. ##### **Finite-Sample and Communication-Efficient Networked Information Aggregation**

In the finite-sample protocol, the message sent along each edge is the agent's prediction vector on a shared sample, so the communication cost is linear in the sample size $m$. In this paper we study this model under communication constraints.
  First, we revisit the finite-sample analysis of the full-message protocol. We identify a gap in the finite-sample proof of Kearns et al. (2026) and develop a different argument that establishes a finite-sample population-risk guarantee along paths satisfying deterministic $M$-coverage.

We analyze this protocol through a reduction to least squares on the compressed sample together with an oblivious subspace embedding argument. The resulting bound separates statistics from communication: the statistical term is still governed by the original sample size $m$, while the additional error due to compression is controlled by the sketch dimension $k$. For Gaussian sketches, we obtain an explicit high-probability population-risk bound, showing that row subsampling is not the only possible communication-statistics tradeoff for networked information aggregation.

The mixing operator, however, has remained softmax attention, inherited from sequence modeling rather than derived from denoising. For Gaussian noise, the optimal denoiser admits a canonical expansion in polynomial functionals of the observation, the Wiener--Volterra series. We prove that softmax attention, and any normalized mixing rule (including linear attention), cannot realize any polynomial map beyond an affine one. In contrast, Volterra layers realize this class directly and, on a data manifold, achieve approximation rates governed by the manifold dimension rather than the ambient pixel dimension. We therefore propose the architecture these results prescribe; raw image patches passed through a bottleneck embedding, followed by cascaded low-rank Volterra layers, which carry no activation function because each layer is nonlinear in its own right. To our knowledge this is the first pixel-space diffusion architecture whose mixing operators are Volterra layers throughout, with no attention, no convolution, no pretrained components, and no activation function on the residual path. 摘要：

##### **Are These Programmers Ripe Enough to Eat? Evaluating How LLMs Handle Lexical Knowledge Conflicts in Translation**
2611.09785v1 by Dongxu Wu, Sara Court, Micha Elsner

External lexical resources can help large language models (LLMs) translate texts in low-resource languages and specialized domains, but these resources may conflict with models' prior lexical knowledge. To isolate this conflict, we construct a controlled Mandarin-to-English translation task in which familiar source words are assigned new meanings within and across semantic categories. We treat these prompt-supplied meanings as contextual knowledge (CK) and systematically evaluate the effects of word frequency, sentence context, and prompt framing on the translated outputs. Across nine models, CK reliance is greater for less frequent source words and in sentence contexts that support the supplied meanings. Prompt framing can reduce CK reliance, but seven of nine models favor CK even when explicitly instructed to ignore the supplied meanings. Within model families, larger models are more likely than smaller models to utilize CK in their outputs. Three follow-up experiments further show that modal weakening lowers CK reliance, the effect of repetition depends on prompt layout, and the effect of reasoning varies across models. Overall, our controlled study reveals how CK reliance varies across conditions and models, motivating new methods to detect and evaluate lexical conflicts and guide models' lexical choices in LLM-based translation.

#### Abstracts
##### **LUCQUE: A lung cancer screening dataset from Québec for reliable AI evaluation**
2611.12817v1 by Johanna Topalis, Yannick Lemaréchal, Gabriel Couture, Hanife Ersay, Loran Nas, Balthasar Schachtner, Bastian Oliver Sabel, Michael Ingrisch, Philippe Després, Katharina Jeblick

BACKGROUND: AI-based tools are expected to play a key role in lung cancer screening (LCS). The evaluation of these tools often relied on private datasets or fragmented public datasets with inconsistent availability of clinical data and expert nodule annotations. This has hindered reproducibility, comparability, and independent assessment of AI tools. To address these gaps, we present LUCQUE, a novel, large, comprehensively annotated dataset derived from LDCT lung cancer screening in Québec, which is accessible for reliable AI evaluation.
  METHODS: LUCQUE was curated and is hosted on PARADIM, a digital infrastructure supporting DICOM data de-identification, annotation, long-term storage and analysis. LDCT scans and clinical information were combined to a curated collection and complemented by voxel-level, expert-derived pulmonary nodule and cyst segmentations, and Lung-RADS-based lesion characteristics. Key dataset characteristics and statistics were reported, including participant demographics, imaging parameters, radiological findings, and segmented lesion characteristics. Dataset analyses were conducted according to PARADIM's model-to-data approach, in which user code is executed on the platform such that data remains on premise.
  RESULTS: LUCQUE currently comprises 1,678 LDCT baseline studies from 1,678 participants. Most studies were classified as Lung-RADS 2. A total of 1,776 pulmonary nodules and 60 cysts were annotated, with a median (interquartile range) volume of $89\,(56,\,159)\,\mathrm{mm}^3$ and $1,384\,(589,\,3793)\,\mathrm{mm}^3$ respectively.
  CONCLUSIONS: Compared to publicly available LCS datasets, LUCQUE currently represents the largest accessible annotated real-world LCS dataset. Access to LUCQUE is provided via a model-to-data approach, enabling privacy-preserving, reproducible, and independent real-world evaluation of AI tools in LCS.

摘要：

##### **LogCDM: Interpretable Log Anomaly Detection via Behavioral Graph Drift and Adaptive Signal Fusion**
2611.12775v1 by Suryansh Talukdar, Taha Sarwat, Jin Soung Yoo

Log-based anomaly detection is increasingly dominated by large pretrained models, from BERT-scale encoders to 8B-parameter language models, which require GPUs at inference time and are difficult to deploy on the monitoring nodes where logs are produced. We present LogCDM, a compact detector that scores each log session using three complementary signals: the next-event surprise of a small causal Transformer, the distance between a graph-attention embedding of the session's own transition graph and that of a normal reference graph, and a transition-drift statistic that we show is exactly a frequency-weighted chi-squared divergence between transition kernels. The divergence view yields an asymptotic null distribution, a length-standardized score, and a per-edge decomposition that explains each alarm. A sigmoid gate fuses the three signals; under a shared-covariance Gaussian model, this gate is the Bayes posterior, and its learned weights indicate which signal each dataset relies on. With 8.1-8.8M parameters and no GPU required at inference, LogCDM reaches F1 scores of 0.984 on HDFS, 0.870 on chronologically split BGL, and 0.850 on Thunderbird, within 0.013-0.12 of an 8.1B-parameter LLM detector evaluated under the same protocol and above all compact baselines on BGL. It scores a session in 5.5-10.5 ms on a CPU. Ablations show that the structural signals add 0.025-0.030 F1 on BGL and Thunderbird but almost nothing on HDFS, consistent with the learned fusion weights.

##### **Library of Hands: Fix the Hands, Not the World Model**
2611.12678v1 by Hasaan Ahmad, Qizhen Ying, Yuxuan Sun, Tianjin Huang, Guangming Wang

A world model predicts how a scene changes after an action, and a planner uses it to search for actions that reach a goal. If the camera moves or the controller changes, the same command moves the robot differently: when its commands are turned by 90 degrees, a frozen planner with the LeWM world model that succeeds in 77.5% of episodes succeeds in only 7.8%. Current fixes retrain the model, need training data, or forget a set-up when it returns. We propose Library of Hands (LoH), a training-free method that keeps the world model frozen and corrects only the map from commands to applied actions, which we call the hand. LoH reads the hand through the frozen model, with no extra sensor: it finds the action that best explains the change between two camera images (a reading). It stores every hand it identifies, so a returning set-up is recognised rather than re-learnt. In closed-loop tests, LoH more than doubles success on three simulated tasks, from 28.6% to 62.3%, and loses only 0.6 percentage points when nothing changes. It also works, unchanged, with two other world models and on two of three models pretrained on real robot data, and its gain grows with the quality of its readings.

We ask whether such tools can be carried to other models through a map between residual streams fit only on unlabeled prompts. We transfer a refusal direction, tested causally by addition and ablation, and two read-out tools, a correctness direction and a sentiment probe, across 110 pairs of models from five families and 1B to 72B parameters and between base and instruction-tuned models, with controls for specificity and five seeds. How much transfers depends on what the map was fit on. A map fit on ordinary questions recovers 54\% of the target's own refusal steering and 16\% to 77\% of its correctness gain; adding unlabeled prompts from the region where the tool operates raises these to 102\% and 88\% to 98\%, while control directions recover at most 1\%. ##### **The balance between feature learning and collapse in dynamical systems-based generative models**
2611.12156v1 by Julian Brandon, Bruno Loureiro, N Alex Cayco-Gajic, Arthur Pellegrino

Over the past decade, a growing body of work has established that implicit biases in learning dynamics fundamentally shape the solutions found by neural networks, governing their learned features and generalisation performance. However, despite these strides in deep learning theory, far less is known about representation learning in generative models based on dynamical systems, from normalising flows to diffusion. Importantly, empirical evidence suggests that continuous-time generative models can be prone to mode collapse, yet a learning theory explaining such biases remains elusive. To address this, we use tools from dynamical systems and operator theory to express the gradient flow of a general class of stochastic differential equations as a composition of linear operators, allowing us to relate collapse to low-rank biases in generative models. In variational inference and flow-matching tasks, we show that this bias can manifest in two different mechanisms: i) where collapsed models are stable fixed points of the gradient flow, or ii) in sequential feature learning, where smaller modes take progressively longer to be learned, producing an effective collapse for a fixed training budget. Together these findings point to a trade-off between feature learning and collapse, suggesting an operator view as a step towards a unified framework for understanding the learning dynamics of dynamical systems-based generative models.

Two laws follow. The depth law is a known result of logic, which we state for these layers: L layers over two-step walks reach exactly 2^L - 1 steps, just as a logical statement with L nested clauses of the form "there is a vertex such that" sees exactly that far. We build a construction that reaches this far, checked in Lean, and, when vertices carry no names, no such network reaches further. The size law is the cost of reading at a temperature, which logic does not have: existence is read correctly at every size, in the worst case, if and only if inverse temperature times margin is at least the logarithm of the number of candidates. ##### **HiddenPathQA: A Benchmark for Knowledge Graph Question Answering When Questions Hide Their Paths**

(iii) We conduct a pseudoword stress test that replaces entity names with meaningless placeholders to distinguish KG-grounded reasoning from entity-level memorization. Holistic Path Selection outperforms six strong KGQA baselines (ToG, PoG, FiDeLiS, R2-KG, KAPING, and KARPA) by 8.8-17.9 accuracy points across two LLM backbones and both original and pseudoword settings. 2611.11224v1 by Idan Refaeli, Yizhak Yisrael Elboher, Guy Katz, Shahaf Bassan

Post-hoc explanation methods for neural networks are often heuristic and lack provable guarantees. A common alternative to such methods that was studied in recent years is to compute a cardinally-minimal subset of input features that is provably sufficient to determine the model's prediction. For general neural networks, however, finding such explanations is computationally intractable. In contrast, recent work showed that this problem becomes more tractable for a particular family of neural networks, namely neural additive models (NAMs), though standard NAMs are highly restrictive because they exclude feature interactions altogether. In this work, we study the computation of provably minimal and sufficient explanations for neural networks with restricted feature interactions, bridging the gap between fully general neural networks and purely additive models. Under either of these settings, we develop algorithms that compute provable explanations for neural networks with any-order interactions while retaining much of the computational efficiency of additive models. Empirically, we show that these explanations are significantly smaller and faster to obtain than those produced by standard algorithms, while being derived from models that achieve much higher accuracy than standard NAMs. Overall, our results help expand the class of neural networks that admit efficient provable explanations, while clarifying the fundamental role of feature interactions in shaping their complexity.

Human-AI complementarity captures the idea that a human assisted by an AI system can outperform both the human and the AI acting alone. Many human-AI interactions, including those where medical AI provides treatment recommendations, however, concern tasks other than predicting what obtains. Because the construction of adequacy labels embeds evaluative choices, we draw methodological inspiration from computational reliabilism to identify technical, scientific, and social factors relevant to supporting their construction. ##### **Inference for Reasoning Models: Practical Lessons from Evaluation and Serving**

Running a language model involves more than its trained weights. The remaining chapters provide a practical reference for reward design, evaluation, training tool-using agents, debugging common failures, reproducibility, rollout engines, and hardware. Written for researchers and practitioners working with open models, the book aims to help readers understand how to train models, recognize when something goes wrong, and decide what to try next.

##### **Update Slope Is Bounded by Prior Calibration: A Structural Bound and Its Contingent Empirical Signature**
2611.10325v1 by Ben Jenkins, Mihaela Cardei

Evaluations of LLM forecasters increasingly measure how forecasts improve as richer evidence is supplied. We identify two confounds in this update-slope metric. First, outcome-aware evidence construction substantially inflates measured responsiveness: on the same models and questions, slopes are roughly four times larger than with resolution-blinded evidence ladders. Second, update slope is a change score with a prior-dependent structural ceiling, so raw comparisons can reflect differences in starting calibration rather than updating behavior. The ceiling defines only a feasible region; whether slope and prior calibration are empirically coupled depends on headroom utilization and varies across our Manifold, ForecastBench, and ACLED pools. Despite this variability, conditioning on prior calibration roughly triples the association between intermediate updating and final Brier score under both blinded and outcome-aware construction. Question identity also explains substantially more variance than model identity across the evaluated pools. We recommend building evidence ladders blind to outcomes, conditioning on prior calibration, and reporting headroom-normalized measures alongside raw slopes.

##### **Key Institutional Considerations for Designing Effective Independent Verification Organizations**

Independent verification organizations (IVOs) have emerged as a popular, bipartisan solution for facilitating high-quality independent evaluations of AI technologies. But momentum is outpacing design: there is emerging consensus on the necessary operating conditions for robust IVOs, but little discourse on institutional and policy specifications to ensure those conditions are met. IVOs are currently envisioned primarily as a mechanism operating at the AI frontier and model level to evaluate catastrophic risks. Little work has yet been done on how to establish or enforce an IVO ecosystem that is equipped to handle myriad risks (i.e., beyond catastrophic ones) and operate at the system and application level across the deployment and use stages of the AI lifecycle. This paper argues that IVO design should ensure the capacity for evaluating AI models, systems, and applications in context for both frontier companies and enterprise companies & AI-adopting organizations. Additionally, this paper provides early considerations to advance IVO discourse from normative to operative, and from existential to everyday risks. Drawing from existing institutional constructs in financial services, education, public health, and science, we outline key considerations on whom IVOs serve, what access they require, how they interact with external governing structures, how they are funded, and how they fit into the wider assurance ecosystem. Much of the structure and knowledge necessary to holistically design IVOs and their ecosystem does not yet exist, even as companies rush to ink agreements with their chosen evaluators.

2611.09891v1 by Peter Belcak, Greg Heinrich, Saurav Muralidharan, Shizhe Diao, Pavlo Molchanov

Large language models reinvent the wheel all the time. They conflate task-solving with the execution of the solutions, which forces them to solve every problem from scratch. This work presents \textbf{PHISHREV+}, a hybrid framework that instantiates the notion of \textit{post-hoc defeasible machine learning}. This framework does not replace the classifier. In the machine learning phase, four classifiers (SVM, KNN, DT and RF) are trained on the 87 feature benchmark dataset. In the post-hoc NMR phase, four defeasible rules derived from exploratory data analysis of the training set, and validated through Chi-square and Mann-Whitney U tests, formally revise initial predictions. ##### **Value-oriented policy distillation for interpretable wind farm power control**

Wind farm power control can help the system maintain balance and avoid blackouts. Thereby, the wind farm helps restore system balance while also reducing its own imbalance costs resulting from forecast errors. However, implicit balancing is a challenging problem since the settlement price is unknown in advance and can be impacted by the pursued control strategy. Therefore, we propose a policy distillation framework using explainable boosting machines (EBMs), a variant of generalized additive models, to obtain a transparent control policy. To increase the value captured by the EBM model, we introduce a value-oriented perspective to policy distillation inspired by research in the field of energy forecasting. Furthermore, we suggest to include expert knowledge during model design, such that the control strategy is also justifiable to stakeholders. ##### **Value or Attention: Investigating Layer-wise Relevance Propagation for Graph Attention Networks**

##### **A Fine-Grained Hierarchy and Benchmark for State Tracking in Language Models**
2611.03094v1 by Jackson Petty, William Merrill, Ashish Sabharwal

Theoretical limitations of transformers on state tracking---the ability to track information about entities or variables over long contexts---have motivated new recurrent language modeling (LM) architectures such as Gated DeltaNet (GDN). These architectures have, in particular, been benchmarked against the $S_5$ word problem, a theoretically hard state-tracking task that captures abilities such as code evaluation under arbitrary variable assignment. It, however, remains open whether other, simpler forms of state tracking might both be more relevant in practice and already explain observed differences between these architectures. We propose evaluating state tracking using a hierarchy of word problems with four increasing levels of difficulty: r-trivial, corresponding to immutable assignment to variables in programming; aperiodic, where assignments are mutable; soluble, where assignments are self-referential; and general finite state tracking. Theoretically, we show that the class of problems transformers are thought to length-generalize on does not even contain all aperiodic state tracking. On the other hand, we prove that GDN with positive eigenvalues expresses all aperiodic problems, and the regular languages recognized by certain transformers are precisely all soluble ones, thus providing a fine-grained picture of state tracking capabilities of various architectures. Taking these ideas to practice, we construct StateBench, a 4-tier long-horizon state-tracking benchmark. Our systematic direct training experiments across architectures largely align with theoretical expectation, albeit with interesting observations at very large scale length-generalization. Additionally, we also evaluate pretrained models---both open hybrid and closed frontier ones---on StateBench, finding that none of them robustly solves even soluble state tracking, suggesting a direction for future improvement.

##### **Spectral Degeneration of Self-Attention under Isotropic Score Geometry**

We study the spectral structure of the self-attention matrix in Transformer models through an idealized spectral model for the pre-softmax score matrix. ##### **When Prediction Shapes the Future: A BlueSky Agenda for Reflexive Time Series Analysis**
2611.02511v1 by Tao Xie, Yiqun Zhang, Yiu-ming Cheung

Time-series models are increasingly deployed in operational systems (e.g., inventory management and clinical care) where predictions trigger interventions that alter future observations, producing records we term AI-shaped trajectories. This sequence-prediction coupling deepens as such systems evolve into agents that autonomously predict, act, and remember across long horizons. Such AI-shaped trajectories support immediate performance monitoring, but turn ambiguous once reused as passive data. An algorithm may then misread a prediction-prevented stockout as naturally stable demand, or an averted medical crisis as a false alarm. This ambiguity then propagates into everything built on the record, from retraining and agent memory to downstream predictive tasks. As a result, systems may internalize these intervention effects as natural phenomena, drifting further from the truth at every closed-loop cycle. In this Blue Sky paper, we advocate an agenda for reflexive time-series analysis that shifts the analytical object from the intervention-agnostic sequence to the prediction-action-observation loop. The agenda requires closed-loop provenance to track the operational context (e.g., predictions, policies, and actions), sequence identification to estimate how those actions affect future values under explicit causal assumptions, and closed-loop evaluation to separate intrinsic process prediction from policy-driven effects. This foundation is essential for integrating autonomous agents into such loops at scale, enabling the joint learning of intervention effects and intrinsic dynamics for robust temporal representations.

Across FrankaKitchen, AntMaze, and Adroit tasks, ANCHOR substantially reduces catastrophic failure while maintaining strong asymptotic performance. Ablations show that KL annealing and the residual conservative weight are important for balancing early stability and online adaptation, while additional analysis shows that ANCHOR is most beneficial when the pretrained policy contains useful behavior to preserve. Code is available at https://github.com/Cho-Geonwoo/ANCHOR.

##### **Machine Learning Beyond Statistical Consistency for Hybrid AI**
2611.01914v1 by Jinsook Kim, Jinho Kang

We propose a new definition of machine learning, according to which a machine learns a function when it computes the function successfully, that is, when the value it returns for each input has a proper derivation in a formal system. We extend Gödel's Representability Theorem, which equates computation with derivation, to empirical targets in two ways: the theorem is extended to the computable reals, and the data are admitted into the system as empirical definitions that record what has been observed. We prove that a machine learns a true probability if and only if two conditions are met. The Truth Condition is that its estimate converges to the true probability, which is statistical consistency. The Assurance Condition is that the machine properly derives its own convergence inside the system. Learning thus lies beyond statistical consistency, for it requires a derivation of the convergence from the record. A global assumption about the data, such as the i.i.d. assumption, does not supply such a derivation, because it leads only to a circular one, as we show for PAC learning. On the other hand, the two conditions are met for the probability of a sentence over a finite ideal corpus. A real machine can approximate a true probability only if an ideal machine can learn it precisely, that is, only in a domain that meets the two conditions. Modern AI works in such a domain, and we offer this as a clue to its success. Modern AI is hybrid as well, in two senses: First, it performs two kinds of tasks, namely probabilistic ones such as predicting the next word and deductive ones such as proving and coding. Second, it combines two parts of different kinds, a statistical proposer and a deductive verifier, for proving and coding. Our framework explains both senses of hybridity in one formal system.

##### **Special and General Computational Aesthetics**
2611.01626v1 by Ganqing Luo, Chuan Li, Liaoruxing Zhang, Xin Jin

Computational aesthetics is a field at the intersection of computer science, artificial intelligence, aesthetics, and other disciplines. As AI has developed, the field has formed relations of inclusion, intersection, or support with computational art, AI art, AI aesthetics, AI-generated content (AIGC), computational creativity, affective computing, and neuroaesthetics. Without distinguishing scope, objectives, and methods, it may be narrowed to image aesthetic quality assessment or expanded into an umbrella term for AI's involvement in art.
  We analyze its internal structure and its relations with neighboring concepts, and propose a two-level scope: special computational aesthetics (SCA) and general computational aesthetics (GCA). SCA centers on aesthetic measurement and aesthetic process modeling. The former primarily corresponds to "Aesthetic Perception": computationally identifying and representing aesthetic attributes and predicting human aesthetic evaluations; the latter primarily to "Aesthetic Understanding": computationally describing, testing, and explaining how human aesthetic perception and judgment are formed. GCA extends this core to include "Aesthetic Generation," namely artistic generation guided by computational aesthetic models. Some computational aesthetic tasks can also use non-AI methods. Research in this scope using AI algorithms and models we define as AI aesthetics, which may involve the special core and the general extension. "Aesthetic Generation" intersects with AIGC, AI art, and computational creativity, but belongs to the general extension defined here only when computational aesthetic models substantively constrain, guide, or optimize artwork generation. We also draw on Feng Peng's discussion of the computability of beauty, aesthetic experience, and art as a theoretical reference for clarifying boundaries between computational aesthetics and related concepts.

##### **Meta-memorization and memorization scaling laws in transformers**
2611.01564v1 by Alex Nguyen, Kenneth A. Norman, Gautam Reddy

Larger transformers can memorize more data, with a capacity (in bits) that scales roughly linearly with the number of parameters. Counterintuitively, larger models also memorize faster, requiring fewer repetitions of a sequence during training before it is memorized. We reproduce both memorization scaling laws in transformers trained on random binary sequences, showing that rapid memorization can arise even when data contain no semantic or compositional structure. Rapid memorization is due to a meta-memorization effect, where models trained to memorize a large set of random sequences learn to memorize novel sequences more quickly. The rate $r(t)$ at which models memorize grows with training time $t$ as a power-law $r(t) \sim t^β$ with a fixed exponent $β$ but an increasing upper cutoff across model scales. Meta-memorization enables even one-layer transformers to few-shot memorize a 100-bit sequence. This effect is partly explained by the formation of structured attention heads that convey position-invariant sequence information to MLPs where memorized sequences are stored. Meta-memorization is recapitulated in a simplified model of a dense associative memory where retrieval is constrained by an attentional bottleneck. Our results suggest transformers, through pre-training, acquire reusable mechanisms that enable highly efficient storage and retrieval of memories.

##### **Vocabulary Aligned Explanations for Computer Vision Models**
2611.01391v1 by Erfan Mirhaji, Nikhil Krishnaswamy, Jill Zarestky, Lisa Mason, Nathaniel Blanchard, Sarath Sreedharan

Optimizing vision models for accuracy does not force the model to learn and utilize human-understandable and domain-specific concepts and rules. We propose explaining image classifications through domain-specific concepts, visual evidence, and expert knowledge expressed as logical rules. We introduce FLUENT (Framework for Localized, Uncertainty-Aware Explanations in Normative Terminology), a post-hoc framework that ranks expert-authored rules combining jointly required, alternative, and absent concepts. To account for prediction errors, FLUENT combines predicted labels and concept states with empirically estimated likelihoods and concept priors. It scores each rule by calculating support over satisfying assignments to the concepts mentioned in that rule, then normalizes these scores to express relative confidence among candidate explanations. The selected rule is illustrated through occlusion-based highlights for positive concept mentions in the query image and localized examples in retrieved reference images for negative mentions. These illustrations are intended to show end users unfamiliar with the domain what the concepts named in an explanation look like. FLUENT combines domain knowledge and model accuracy, and delivers visual concept highlights and explicit rule confidence to explain an image in the vocabulary of an application domain.

##### **Are Fake Citations a Form of Reward Hacking? A Pre-Registered Study of Post-Training and Citation Cues**
2611.01313v1 by Zain Naboulsi

Post-trained language models fabricate citations at high rates. We ask whether that is reward hacking: scoring well on a proxy for scholarship while doing worse on what it stands for. We ran four pre-registered experiments on 900 frozen questions (US federal case law, computationally adjacent scientific literature, and medical literature) across twelve open-weights checkpoints covering four post-training stages of three model families, then refit on fifteen. The clearest result holds the weights fixed and changes only the prompt. Asking for citations raises fabrication sharply in post-trained checkpoints and not in base ones: a 47.8-point interaction on twelve checkpoints (Holm-adjusted p = 2e-34) and 41.5 points on fifteen, in all three domains. A length-matched control separates the citation cue from a demand for a longer answer, so the effect is specific to citations, not verbosity. Post-training also raises fabrication overall, from 4.8% to 56.7%, but our registered prediction that it would rise most where fabrication is most tempting did not hold. A preference model trained only on surface citation features prefers post-trained output 86% of the time, and holding citation count fixed shows it rewards quantity. Our answer: possibly, but not proven. Post-trained models produce fabricated citations at far higher rates when asked for sources, and a naive evaluator that counts sources without checking them would prefer their output for citing more. But the targeted pattern we registered did not appear, and plain instruction-following could explain the same results. Proving that the reward causes fabrication needs an experiment that changes the reward during training, which this study does not do. Pre-registration: doi:10.17605/OSF.IO/S53HY. Code and data: https://github.com/zainnab-sparq/citation-reward-hacking

##### **Toward Bounding Coding-Agent Autonomy in Software Maintenance: A Pilot Study of Boundary Contracts**
2611.01265v1 by Heeyong Eun, Seungyeon Chung, Hyejee Park, Youngeun Lee, Sukhyun Hong, Hyewon Jung

As coding agents gain greater autonomy to modify software repositories, relevant context alone does not define their operational authority. An artifact may be relevant to a maintenance task while being inappropriate to edit; conversely, another artifact may be required even when it is not explicitly mentioned in the request. We explore Boundary Contracts, a machine-readable abstraction that makes task-specific artifact roles (editable, inspect-only, required, and protected) explicit before repository modification begins.
  We conduct a pilot study on 40 maintenance tasks from a real enterprise repository, comparing the same Codex-based coding agent with and without Boundary Contract information. The boundary-informed condition improves macro edit-selection F1 from 0.440 to 0.682, required-behavior coverage from 0.429 to 0.673, and the number of strictly acceptable patches from 9 of 40 to 18 of 40. To probe whether these gains are explained only by improved file localization, we additionally analyze 21 tasks in which both conditions modify at least one identical file. Differences remain in edit selectivity and patch outcomes, providing initial evidence that explicit artifact roles may influence maintenance behavior beyond localization alone.
  This pilot does not establish the general effectiveness of Boundary Contracts. Rather, it provides initial evidence for a broader research question: how should the operational boundaries of coding agents be generated, validated, revised, and ultimately enforced as repository-level maintenance becomes increasingly autonomous?

##### **A Token Is Not a Unit of Difficulty: Segmentation Controls Where Adaptive Computation Goes Inside a Word**
2611.00983v1 by Dipto Sumit, Mahima Mahzabin Sandria, Farig Sadeque, Md. Golam Rabiul Alam

Early-exit language models decide for each token how much computation to spend, usually by stopping once the prediction is confident. ##### **Sparse Autoencoder Attribution Graphs Are Decided at the Margin**
2611.00606v1 by Karl Li

Attribution graphs over the features of sparse autoencoders (SAEs) are used to predict what an ablation does and are compared across training seeds. On five public TopK SAE pairs from three Pythia models, zeroing one feature moves a next layer feature across the cutoff in 0.74 of cases or more, and the crossings carry most of the response's energy. ##### **WAR-CRAFT: Refining Web API Recommendations through Requirement Auditing**
2611.00564v1 by Zishuo Xu, Dezhong Yao, Zhiqiang Xu

Web API recommenders return a set of APIs for a mashup request but do not check it against provider documentation, the main public evidence of an API's capabilities. The set can therefore contain unsupported or redundant APIs and miss requested capabilities. Filling every apparent gap does not fix this, because a gap may already be served, may not need its own API, or may have no documented provider. We study post-recommendation refinement, which revises a frozen recommender's output using only the request, the catalog, and provider documentation. We propose WAR-CRAFT, which treats refinement as three decisions. An LLM removes unsupported or subsumed APIs by judging candidates jointly, then audits the remaining set against a requirement checklist derived from the request. For each uncovered requirement, a fine-tuned catalog-constrained Resolver proposes a catalog API or abstains, and an independent documentation check admits only supported proposals. On ProgrammableWeb with 7 heterogeneous upstream recommenders, WAR-CRAFT raises mean SetF1 from 0.558 to 0.644, above the strongest of 5 refinement baselines (62.5%), and roughly halves functional redundancy (0.127 to 0.065) without changing mean functional coverage. Gains range from +2.2 points on generative recommenders to +18.2 on fixed-cutoff rankers. At matched set sizes, WAR-CRAFT still outperforms truncating the upstream output, so the gain is not explained by set-size calibration alone. Our code is available at https://github.com/ZsXu-enrico/WAR-CRAFT.

##### **Faster Imitation by Re-timing Demonstrations Within the Controller Limit**
2611.00539v1 by Zongtan Li, Tianming Liu, Ye Zhao

Imitation policies inherit the operator's pace, and re-timing the demonstrations to run faster works only until success collapses at two to three times demonstration speed. Recent methods attribute that collapse to distribution shift and answer it with finer rules for which segments may be sped up; we show it has a mechanical cause that segment selection cannot address. A low-level controller realises only a bounded displacement per control period, so merging consecutive commands produces sums outside that bound: training pairs that promise a transition the plant cannot deliver. The share of such pairs in the re-timed data, which we call the exceed share, tracks where the retrained policy collapses. The rule that follows has a single parameter: merge greedily while the sum stays realisable. Every command is then realisable by construction, the demonstrated geometry is preserved, and the speed-up lands only where the demonstrator left slack. On four simulated robomimic tasks, 200 held-out seeds per cell, a merge cap of two is at or above the frozen policy's success on all four, by 14 points on Square, and shortens the successful episodes. At matched tempo, uniform re-timing loses 9 and 16 points on Can and ToolHang, is within noise on the other two, and reaches zero on the hardest at 2x. Placement also matters: no shuffled, entropy-guided or speed-conditioned alternative beats the rule on three of the four tasks, by margins the exceed share alone does not explain; on the fourth, at a tempo the task absorbs, every placement lands in the same band. The same principle carries to a simulated humanoid whole-body tracker: re-timing 429 reference motions within its velocity envelope succeeds on 71% of clips at 2.3x, against 51% for uniform time scaling at 2x.

##### **Same Question, Different Answers: Auditing Persona-Dependent Variation in Six LLMs on Smoking-Cessation Advice**
2610.42039v1 by Artin Yousefi, Aizierjiang Aiersilan, Lorien C Abroms, David A Broniatowski

Hundreds of millions of people now ask large language model (LLM) chatbots for health advice, which raises a question that is simple to pose and hard to answer: does everyone get the same advice? We audit six widely deployed LLMs from four providers on smoking cessation, a domain with codified clini- cal guidance and substantial public-health stakes, by putting the same four cessation questions to twelve user personas that differ in life stage, self-described expertise, and smoking history, for a total of 2,880 responses. What the models say depends on who appears to be asking. Every model named FDA-approved prescription cessation medications less often to teen personas than to adult personas, by 32 to 67 percentage points, and in five of six models a persona presenting health- care expertise received fewer facts than a persona presenting none. We then evaluate one deployable intervention: a short system prompt specifying a factual floor, the set of core med- ical facts every user should receive when they are relevant, while leaving tone, vocabulary, and framing free to adapt. The prompt narrows persona differences in every model, but its effect on overall fact coverage varies in both magnitude and direction across models, so a prompt validated on one system cannot be assumed to transfer to another. Two cautions frame these results. Equal mention rates are not always the correct clinical target, because prescription cessation pharmacother- apy is not recommended for minors, so a defensible factual floor must be age-aware rather than uniform. And because our personas vary on several attributes at once, we report scenario- level differences rather than isolated effects of age or expertise. We release all 2,880 responses and the analysis code.
